sion (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 6); however, all the rights that other
employees enjoy are also valid for prostitutes. This started the process of equaliza-
tion. Many problems and injustices are still present and up to this point, the strive
toward equalization has been a very long journey.
Chapter 3
A Meta-Model




At ﬁrst glance, the market for commercial sex does not seem that different from
any other market. Supply and demand interact and determine the price and quan-
tity of services sold. However, no other market is, in fact, quite like the one for
prostitution. This is not only because women sell a service that is one of the most
private actions for the majority of people – one can argue that all of us, some more
than the others, sell something we do with our body (Nussbaum, 1999, p. 276).
The bricklayer builds a wall with the strength of his arms; the teacher stands in
front of a class writing, explaining, and gesticulating; the researcher contemplates
various kind of problems; the prostitute sells, quite frankly, sex.
    What exactly then is the difference between the market for commercial sex and
all other labor markets? One of the most cited publications on this topic suggests
that
   "Prostitution has an unusual feature: it is well paid despite being lowskill, labor intensive,
   and, one might add, female dominated. Earnings even in the worst-paid type, streetwalk-
   ing, may be several multiples of full-time earnings in professions with comparable skill
   requirements." (Edlund and Korn, 2002, p. 182)

Many people probably agree with Edlund and Korn (2002) that women do not need
a usual skill set to work in prostitution. However, one cannot resist the impression
that Edlund and Korn (2002, pp. 188) are rather talking about the education of
women offering sex work than they talk about the actual demands the profession
prostitution calls for.100 Furthermore, we argue that none of the explanations pro-
posed are why commercial sex is so different from any other market – they are, at
100 The ﬁrst question is how to deﬁne low-skilled. The U.S. Department of Education gives the

following deﬁnition: "Individuals who have attained 16 years of age; who are not enrolled or
required to be enrolled in secondary school under state law; and who lack sufﬁcient mastery of
basic educational skills to enable the individuals to function effectively in society; do not have a
secondary school diploma or its recognized equivalent, and have not achieved an equivalent level

Eva-Maria Heberer, Prostitution, DOI 10.1007/978-3-658-04496-1_3,
© Springer Fachmedien Wiesbaden 2014
74                                                                              3 A Meta-Model

most, outcomes of this unique position. Instead, the unique characteristic we as-
sert is that no other market is so much tabooed like the market for commercial sex.
Norms and morality inﬂuence many markets very differently, but they are seldom
so closely linked to stigmatization. With each policy reform, the government gives
a moral direction for the treatment of prostitution. A moral standard that condemns
prostitution leads to a higher stigmatization of sex workers if a woman is caught –
this we call the possible loss of reputation. A loss of reputation means that women
lose their standing in society, or at least must live with a moral stigma after being
caught offering sexual services.
    According to theory, the loss of reputation should be included in the price for
services sold, as women bearing a higher risk should be compensated through
a higher wage. During the past decades, the intensity of this moral stigma has
diminished. The same act that used to exclude women from society 100 years ago
may still not be accepted, but is at least tolerated. Accordingly, we should observe
different prices and a different supply of commercial sex for the decades analyzed.
The aim of this work is to analyze how changes in working conditions affect the
situation on the market for commercial sex services. If we can show that legal
and moral changes had an inﬂuence on the price and supply of commercial sex in
Germany during the last 167 years, we might also be able to comment on current
legislative changes in Europe. With that, policy implications and recommendations
should not be too far away anymore.
    In contrast to other publications in this ﬁeld, we focus entirely on explaining the
supply of commercial sex and leave the discussion about the behavior of the de-
mand side as well as the derivation of an equilibrium for further research. Edlund
and Korn (2002), for instance, go a step further and model a market equilibrium.
Unfortunately, the decision of women to engage as a supplier remains rather vague
in this publication. To us, the decision for men to demand commercial sex seems
rather obvious: Besides demanding the explicit good of commercial sex, they also

of education; or are unable to speak, read, or write the English language" (cited from Park et al.,
2007, p. 3). A German study uses a deﬁnition which describes low-skilled as those people who
have not completed apprenticeship or have not visited higher school (Känel et al., 2008, p. 4). If
asking for school education, SOFFI K. (2005b, p. 129) ﬁnds that of all interviewed prostitutes,
2.4% were still attending school, only 12.0% have not completed school at all, and 25.7% have
ﬁnished traditional secondary modern school. Middle school was completed by 25.3% and 25.7%
passed the ﬁnal secondary-school examination. So far, the women do not seem to differ too
much from the rest of the German population. Weishaupt et al. (2010) ﬁnd that 28.5% of all
German graduates in 2010 were graduating from a traditional secondary modern school, 50.8%
have graduated middle school, and 31.7% have passed the ﬁnal secondary-school examination
(Weishaupt et al., 2010, p. 89). Of all school beginners, 12% did not complete school at all
(Weishaupt et al., 2010, p. 36). SOFFI K. (2005b) is not the only one ﬁnding empirical evidence
contradicting the assumption of Edlund and Korn (2002). For Ireland, O’Conner (1996, p. 5) still
ﬁnds that 39% of the interviewed prostitutes attend school until the age of 18.
3 A Meta-Model                                                                                75

often look for the girlfriend experience – understanding, closeness to a woman, or
simply conversations (see, e.g., Earle and Sharp (2007, p. 59)). However, as none
of the above is incomprehensible to be desired, for many it is not understandable
why women sell such an intimate action. This might also explain why the ratio of
supply and demand are so different: During the 1990s, Die Grünen (1990, p. 5)
claim that two thirds of all men demanded commercial sex in Germany. During
the same time, Stallberg (1988, p. 11) ﬁnds that around 200,000 prostitutes were
active. Taking the female population in Germany for the year 1989 as a bench-
mark,101 this accounts for 0.49% offering commercial sex. Thus, it seems just
reasonable to assume that the supply side of the market is the scarce one and their
decision to engage in the market for commercial sex, accordingly, much more cru-
cial to study. In addition, male participants do not fall victim to the moral stigma-
tization women experience when being honest about their profession. It shall not
be neglected that men also – especially married men – fear detection. However,
the extent of the consequences men and women have to live with is quite different.
All of these arguments are in favor of starting an analysis of the market for com-
mercial sex on the supply side of the market. As a matter of fact, the conclusions
and interpretations of this work can only be understood as valid for the female
decision.
    In order to understand the market for prostitution services, we ﬁrst develop a
short meta-model capturing the relevant driving factors. The aim of a meta-model
is not to specify equations and functions, but rather to see how the market and
its participants behave and what inﬂuences their behavior from a broader point of
view. The advantage of using such an abstract model is that possible shortcomings
of speciﬁcation are not a matter of concern.
    Economists and other researchers alike tend to unnecessarily complicate their
ﬁndings and thoughts. As a matter of fact, publications are not always tractable
and often artiﬁcially mathematized. Here, we try to avoid both and instead follow
the advice given by Blad and Keiding (1990, p. 83):
   "A theory must be general in order to be applicable in as many cases as possible – espe-
   cially so in economics where our a priori knowledge of the phenomena is usually scant –
   but at the same time the theory should lead to statements which put as many restrictions
   as possible on what is going to happen – otherwise the theory becomes useless."

   In fact, we build a model using only budget constraints in order to analyze the
strength of substitution and income effects if parameters change over time. The ad-
vantage of using exclusively budget constraints rather than utility functions lies in
the generality. Deﬁning and specifying utility functions is a valid instrument, how-
ever, one must make strong assumptions in order to do so. Utility functions show
special cases and are not guaranteed true for different agents. Budget constraints,
101 Which was at 41,003,100 women (Statistisches Bundesamt, 1991).
76                                                                    3 A Meta-Model

in contrast, need to be fulﬁlled for all agents and are present in most problems
economists discuss (see, e.g., Hoy (2001, p. 585)). One cannot spend more than
one earns during a lifetime (at least not legally). Neither is it possible to violate
a time constraint since there are only 24 hours per day to allocate to different ac-
tivities. Of course, one can discuss all kinds of details in modeling, but budget
constraints still seem to be the instrument that allows for the most general result.
    We begin by looking at a very general model which develops budget constraints
as straightforward as possible and is inspired by Wickström and Barthel (2005).
The fundamentals for this model can be found in every standard micro book, but
"because it is simple, we can more fully appreciate many of the difﬁcult problems
which arise at this stage" (Takayama, 1985, p. 174). The second step is then to ap-
ply a different model, the one developed by Sandmo (1981). Originally, this model
had the purpose to represent the possible behaviors of tax evaders and became a
standard tool in economics since then. In contrast to the ﬁrst approach, uncertainty
is modeled explicitly here and we modify and rewrite the model to ﬁnd a version
suitable for our needs.
    With both models, we try to answer the question of how changes in law en-
forcement inﬂuence prostitutes’ situation over time, focusing on Germany. Clear
cut results are seldom in economics and we therefore use both models and com-
pare the possible outcomes. With uncertainty entering the agents’ decision, it is, in
fact, reasonable to expect the Sandmo (1981) model to generate results closer to
the considerations of actual sex workers. On the other hand, the fear of being de-
tected in this model is caused by the fear of a penalty levied on the agent. Today’s
sex workers do not need to fear any prosecution if being detected – at least not if
performed outside a prohibition area – and since the ProstG was enacted in 2002,
their profession is equalized to all other professions in Germany. In earlier years,
however, the penalty might have played a role. As a matter of fact, each model
explains different epochs of jurisdiction in Germany. Interestingly, both show very
similar outcomes – an additional argument for looking at both models to prove that
the predictions are reasonable and robust. The next chapter – Chapter 4 – then ap-
plies both models to the history of prostitution. To be able to do that, the following
Sections 3.1 and 3.2 are dedicated to developing the models in detail.



3.1 Building Up from Budget Constraints

When starting to think about modeling the market for commercial sex, one usu-
ally starts by reading the publications of other authors. Among them, Edlund and
Korn (2002), Della Giusta et al. (2009), and Arunachalam and Shah (2008) are
probably the ones most cited within economics. From our point of view, all of the
3.1 Building Up from Budget Constraints                                                 77

mentioned publications contribute to a better understanding of how the market for
commercial sex functions. Because shortcomings are indisputable, we neverthe-
less decided to set up a new model, keeping in mind that less is sometimes more.
As a matter of fact, a standard micro model is used, trying to show the speciﬁcs
of the market without complicating the analysis any more than necessary. In the
following, we model a woman’s decision using budget constraints. In a ﬁrst step, a
one-period model is chosen. In Section 3.1.2, we extend the horizon to include two
time periods. Within both approaches, we decompose the effect in two individual
ones based on the Slutsky decomposition, developed in 1915 by E.E. Slutsky, a
Russian economist.



3.1.1 A One-Period Model

To ﬁnd an easy and comprehensible starting point, we ﬁrst look at a simpliﬁcation
of the later intertemporal model.102 In this very ﬁrst version, we allow for only one
period. Women have different possibilities of how to spend their time. They can
either work in the secure labor market v for a wage a, enjoy leisure F, or work on
the market for commercial sex s earning a wage ps . The 24-hour day is normalized
to one and the general time constraint needs to be fulﬁlled

                                       1 = v + s + F.                                 (3.1)

For simplicity, we assume that leisure is ﬁxed and constant and rewrite equation
3.1 as
                                 1 − F̄ = v + s.                           (3.2)
Working in s is risky and should include some kind of beneﬁt as working there
otherwise would not be an option for a rational agent. Accordingly, we assume
that ps > a is always true: Women earn more working as a prostitute than they
would earn on the regular labor market. The question is what this riskiness implies.
Firstly, we introduce a probability of getting caught: Women will be detected to
offer services on the commercial sex market with a probability ρ, but at the ex-
pense of some kind of reputation. To keep the analysis tractable, we assume that
the loss of reputation r is deﬁned as
                                             s
                                       r=       γρ.                                   (3.3)
                                            v+s


102 Further information and explanations are given in Wickström and Barthel (2005).
78                                                                     3 A Meta-Model

Thus, detection has a consequence for any woman offering commercial sex. The
loss of reputation might include legal ﬁnes or foregone earnings if arrested and
is modeled after Della Giusta et al. (2009). In reality, r is probably much more
complex than any mathematic equation is able to express. One could, for example,
argue that a loss of reputation depends on the status of an individual within society
(see, e.g., Della Giusta et al. (2009)). However, it is likely that people only care
about how their relatives, friends, and acquaintances judge their actions.
   γ tries to capture this by representing the mentality of society: A condemning
society would be characterized by a high γ and a society consisting of tolerant
people by the opposite. Further, we assume that 0 < γ < 1 is always true. Since we
mostly live in peer groups with similar characteristics as ourselves, it might as well
be true that the relevant status is the same for everybody (see, e.g., Galí (1994) and
Barbour et al. (2013)). Furthermore, other variables may be relevant when it comes
to the loss of reputation, including age since society might forgive more easily if
prostitution was the sin of one’s youth as well as education level. However, all these
factors further complicate the already complex analysis. Nevertheless, additional
research might lead to other conclusions.
   Rewriting equation 3.24 for s and substituting into the time constraint given in

possibility of a woman working in s only and fearing no detection at all. Accord-
ingly, it is given at

                                   r̂∗∗ = 1 − γρ.                            (3.22)

    Corner solutions are given at the points B and D in Figures 3.2a and 3.2b. With
a sufﬁciently small ρ, the agent is in point D in Figure 3.2b where she supplies
all her work time on the market for commercial sex and realizes the consumption
level Cmax . Even with a further decreasing ρ, the agent cannot realize a higher con-
sumption level than Cmax and will, therefore, only consume r̂ but not more units
of C. Accordingly, she just remains in the corner solution. The same is true for

B in Figure 3.2a. Assuming an interior solution, the agent would be somewhere
around point E in Figure 3.2c consuming a bundle of C and r̂. Stating the exact
consumption bundle is difﬁcult as the slope of BD could, of course, also be differ-
ent as in the cases shown in Figure 3.2, but never higher than the one of AB. With
a decreasing γρ, the agent moves from point B to point A. Note that in this case –
with a decreasing γρ –, the income effect loses its inﬂuence and the substitution
effect becomes more important. It seems reasonable to assume that in reality, ρ –
the probability of getting caught – is comparatively small and causes a decreasing
income effect. The term ∂∂ r̂I (1− r̂) in equation 3.18 is then less relevant for our con-
siderations and we are left with two, multiplicatively-linked, negative terms. As a
matter of fact, the effect on r̂ should then be positive: An increasing probability of
getting caught has, in this case, a strictly positive inﬂuence.
   Recalling that r̂ is deﬁned as the share of regular work and assuming a time
constraint as given in equation 3.2, this implies a negative effect on the share of
sex work. With two individually negative effects – on r and C –, the overall effect
should also be negative. Up to now, in a one-period model with a risky and secure
working alternative, women supply fewer working hours on the market for com-
mercial sex if the probability of getting caught rises. In Section 3.1.2, we show that
the same reaction has to be expected if women can decide upon consumption and
earnings for two periods.
84                                                                   3 A Meta-Model

3.1.2 A Two-Period Model

In the following, a two-period model is developed. We use the same variables as
in Section 3.1.1, to ensure tractability. However, an Arabic number in the index
of each variable indicates whether it is a decision variable for the ﬁrst or for the
second time period. Although time is in reality a continuous variable, it is modeled
discretely here. By doing so, we assume that the variables change only once per
analyzed interval, which simpliﬁes the entire analysis to a large extent (see, e.g.,
Hoy (2001, p. 754)). We also assume that the market we are looking at is a com-
petitive one, implying that many agents are active. The female decision to enter
the market for commercial sex as a supplier of services is analyzed.
    In period one, each woman decides how much work she offers on the regular
labor market v1 for a wage a and how much of her work time she dedicates to
working in the commercial sex sector s1 for a wage ps . ps > a is still valid as
prostitution is a risky profession and women should be compensated for working in
it. Ergo, it is reasonable to assume that women receive compensation in monetary
terms if offering sexual services. In addition to the income from working, each
women receives exogenous income y1 .
    The possible loss of reputation r1 now consists of the share of hours the respec-
tive woman spends on the commercial sex sector in either period – relative to her
total work supply – as well as the probability of getting caught ρ and the mentality
of society γ. For any period i, we deﬁne ri as
                                            si

                                  1 − F̄i = vi + si ,                         (3.25)

we ﬁnd expressions for both work variables depending on other parameters only.

women do not pay wage taxes for offering sex work as this form of labor is hidden
from society and, thus, without the knowledge of any tax authority. In contrast,
income is always taxed. In period two, the representative woman again allocates
her time between the regular labor market and the commercial sex sector. How-
ever, in addition to her previous earnings, she now has exogenously given returns
z from her savings in period one. The other variables are similar to those pre-

is not necessarily the same commodity as consumption in period two (see, e.g.,
Takayama (1985, p. 169)).104 Accordingly, the budget constraint for the second

Equation 3.33 is the present value of life-time income, which sums up the income
an individual can earn in period one and two discounted for today. This present
value of life-time income must be equal to the present value of consumption, which
is expressed by the right hand side of equation 3.31. One could also think of this
income as wealth as we include the potential work for each consumer – resources
can be goods as well as labor services (see, e.g., Takayama (1985, p. 171)). pC1
from equation 3.34 represents the price for consumption in period one, whereas

In the model at hand, women must be compensated for the inconvenience caused
by working on the market for commercial sex. Thus, ps > a was assumed and the

stitution effects, we ﬁnd the opposite relationship than before: ∂∂pr̂i |Ū and ∂∂pr̂i |Ū
                                                                     r̂1            r̂2
are both negative. With an increasing price of a good, the demand for the same
good needs to decrease in order to hold utility constant. Accordingly, we are left
with negative and positive effects and the total effect on reputation in ambiguous
for the formulation chosen here. One should, however, remember the discussion
from the previous section: For reasonable values of ρ, the income effect loses
3.2 Explicitly Modeling Uncertainty                                                                89

parts of its inﬂuence and only the negative substitution effects remain. From that,
we can conclude that the effect on reputation is rather a positive one. Together with
the necessary time constraint, we then know that the supply of sex work decreases
with an increasing probability of getting caught.
    For both of our variables of interest – Ci and ri –, we ﬁnd a negative effect of
an increasing ρ and the overall outcome should also be negative. This implies that
with an increasing probability of getting caught, we have also found for the two-
period model a negative effect on the supply of work on the commercial sex sector.
A ﬁnal result, however, can only be found with deﬁned utility functions.105 Instead
of expanding the discussion on all the speciﬁcations necessary to implement utility
functions, we leave them for further research. For now, we keep the generality of
the model and focus rather on possible changes in its structure. The model dis-
cussed in the next section models uncertainty explicitly but still uses only general
utility functions instead of deﬁned ones. It rather varies the model so that we have
only one period, but two possible outcomes within this period. We will see at the
end of Section 3.2, that a similar conclusion can be drawn from that when it comes
to effects of a changing probability of getting caught.



3.2 Explicitly Modeling Uncertainty

The model used here is adopted from Sandmo (1981) and modiﬁed for our re-
search question.106 In the original version, Sandmo (1981) analyzes the behavior
of tax evaders. In contrast, we use the model to describe the female supply of com-
mercial sex and the women’s decision how to allocate their work time. The title of
this section already suggests the major difference to the previous model: Here, we
model the uncertainty of being detected explicitly.
    N women are assumed to be living within society. All of them offer work on the
regular labor market and have to pay a tax t for their earnings. In addition, some
women, namely the fraction N u , offer services on the commercial sex sector for
which they only pay taxes if the government detects their side job.107 Detection
is costly for the government and, accordingly, not certain. As a matter of fact,

105 For further reading, one might want to look, for example, at Kreps (1990, pp. 775), Mas-Colell

et al. (1995, pp. 40), or Blad and Keiding (1990, pp. 39).
106 It might be useful to recall the research question again: If particular variables change over time

– such as the loss of reputation and the probability of getting caught – can we make a statement
about how the supply of work on the market for commercial sex changes? If so, are we able to
make predictions about how the quantity will respond to current legal modiﬁcations?
107 We see in the following, that women do not pay an explicit tax for earnings from commercial

sex, but the tax is included in the penalty rate χ we introduce in the following section.
90                                                                  3 A Meta-Model

women have a fair chance of offering commercial sex undetected. The decision
of this u−type woman is discussed in Section 3.2.2. The fraction of women who
do not offer commercial sex at all, type b, and their considerations is the subject
of Section 3.2.1. For simplicity, all agents are identical – implying the same skill
level for all of the members within each group.



3.2.1 The b-Type

To have a benchmark case, we start by looking at the fraction of women N b who do
not engage in the market for commercial sex. Any of the utility functions used here
are assumed to be twice differentiable and strictly quasiconcave. For the b−type
women, the general form can be written as

                                 Ub = U(Cb , Fb )                            (3.49)

with consumption Cb and leisure Fb . Agents wish to maximize their utility func-
tion, but are restricted by a budget constraint. For the representative woman, this
constraint is deﬁned as
                                Cb = y + ab v(1 − t)                        (3.50)
where y represents an exogenously given income and ab is the wage women earn
if working in sector v. We assume that the wage a b−type women earns ab might
differ from a sex worker’s wage a – even if both do, in fact, the same work on
the regular labor market – and that a < ab is always true. Substituting the time
constraint, which again is given by equation 3.2, into equation 3.50 gives us the
budget constraint depending on the opportunity cost of working.

                         y + ab (1 − t) = Cb + (1 − t)ab Fb                  (3.51)

with Fb for the potential leisure time women consume. The left hand side of the
equation shows the overall income a woman of group b has at her disposal. This
income is allocated on her spendings for the numeraire good consumption and
leisure. The price for leisure is equivalent to the opportunity cost of not working.
For simplicity, we can set

                                 I = y + ab (1 − t)                          (3.52)
                                 pFb = (1 − t)ab                             (3.53)

and with that, equation 3.51 reduces to
3.2 Explicitly Modeling Uncertainty                                                 91

                                      I = Cb + pFb Fb .                         (3.54)

Remember that group b only deﬁnes a reference case, which is given here for the
sake of completeness. b-type women do not engage in the market for commercial
sex – the market we are actually interested in. Accordingly, they do not have to
fear any prosecution; the probability of getting caught plays no role in their con-
siderations. Analyzing changes in ρ is therefore impossible for this group.

                                                                  Fb

              Fig. 3.3: Hicks Decomposition for the Benchmark Case


To see the general structure, other changes can also be analyzed for this easier ver-
sion of the model. Thus, we now assume an increase in the work tax t and start
by looking at a simple graphic given in Figure 3.3. The Hicks decomposition is
discussed ﬁrst, the Slutsky case then follows. Assuming that a tax on wage work
is introduced, the relative price for leisure increases. Accordingly, the budget con-
straint rotates inwards, thereby steepening the slope. The agent is now on a lower
indifference curve. To separate the effects, the Hicks decomposition looks at a case
where the utility is kept constant. To reach such a situation, the budget constraint is
shifted parallel to reach the previous utility level. Point D displays this ﬁnal point,
whereas point A marks the starting point. Point B shows the temporary equilibrium
before the parallel shift took place. The income effect is now the movement from
B to D and the substitution effect is shown by the movement from A to D.
92                                                                   3 A Meta-Model

   In contrast to the Hicks decomposition, the Slutsky version shows a situation
in which the agent is again able to buy the bundle she used to consume before the
prices changed. Accordingly, Slutsky keeps the income and Hicks the utility con-
stant (see, for example, Varian (2001, pp. 144)). In fact, if we look at small price
changes, the difference between both versions is rather insigniﬁcant. However, for
the sake of completeness we show the less often used graphic for the Slutsky de-
composition in Figure 3.4. Note that we have one additional indifference curve
here. Remember that in the Hicks decomposition, we shifted the new budget line
upwards to reach the point where the budget line meets the old indifference curve,
as we wanted to keep utility constant. Now, we must shift the budget line even
more as it needs to meet the old equilibrium. A new indifference level can be
reached.108


                   Cb

                  SE
                                                  B
                   IE

after a change in the work tax and generally ﬁnd

                         =−      ab (1 − Fb ) −       | ab ≷ 0.                  (3.58)
                      dt     ∂I                 ∂ pFb Ū

Like consumption, leisure is also a normal good and with that ∂∂FIb > 0 is fulﬁlled.
With a binding time constraint, the entire ﬁrst term is negative due to the sign.
Additionally, with an increasing price for leisure, its consumption must decline to
keep utility constant, making ∂∂pFFb |Ū negative. The second term is thus positive
                                    b
due to its sign. With ab > 0, one negative and one positive effect was identiﬁed.
Accordingly, a clear-cut result is not possible and the outcome depends on the
deﬁnition of the utility function.
   As the effect on consumption was negative and a clear-cut result not possible
for leisure, the overall effect is ambiguous for b−type women facing an increas-
ing tax on wage work. Nevertheless, the preceding section had rather the goal of
introducing a new model and explaining the interactions within it. In the follow-
ing, we focus on u−type women. In contrast to the type we have just discussed,
94                                                                   3 A Meta-Model

u−type women take the possibility to offer sex work into account when deciding
upon their time allocation. For our questions, this case seems to be the crucial one.



3.2.2 The u-Type

After looking at the benchmark case, we now start to include the second type of
work s – work in the commercial sex sector. A N u -sized group of women enjoys
consumption and leisure and prefers not to be detected selling commercial sex. To
include uncertainty in the model, risk must enter the women’s decision and inﬂu-
ence the expected utility. Just like in Section 3.1, we are interested in analyzing
changes in the probability of getting caught, which calls for looking at the form
of the expected utility. For convenience, women maximize a utility function that
is dependent on a ﬁxed amount of leisure F̄ as well as on Ce , consumption in case
of detection, with a probability ρ or on Cn , consumption in the case of working
undetected, with a corresponding probability (1 − ρ). For simplicity, all women
who are not relevant for our considerations are ruled out – we only look at women
who are willing to supply work in the commercial sex sector. With a probability
like the one given here, we need to look at an expected von Neumann-Morgenstern
utility, which can be written as

                      E(U) = ρU(Ce , F̄) + (1 − ρ)U(Cn , F̄)                  (3.59)

with Ci for consumption in either state, i ∈ {e, n}, and F̄ for leisure.
   Two different outcomes – and with that two different budget constraints – are
possible, depending on whether the side job is detected or not. If the woman works
undetected, her budget constraint can be written as

                             Cn = y + av(1 − t) + ps s                        (3.60)

with y for an exogenously given income, s for the hours worked in the commercial
sex sector, and ps for the wage earned there. Only work in v will be taxed with a
rate t, as this income is known to the authorities. A tax for working in s only needs
to be paid if the government investigates and ﬁnds out about the second profession
of the woman. The total income, which includes the exogenous income as well as
the labor income from both sectors, will be spent for consumption Cn .
   If it is discovered that the woman works as a prostitute, her income and with that
the consumption possibilities change. Detected women have to pay a penalty rate
χ, which includes but exceeds the regular wage tax for work s: χ > t. It must be
decided at the beginning of the period where to supply how many hours of work.
3.2 Explicitly Modeling Uncertainty                                                             95

The budget constraint is then given by

                          Ce + ηr = y + av(1 − t) + ps s(1 − χ).                           (3.61)

In comparison to the income given in equation 3.60, the existence of χ ensures that
those women who are detected realize a lower income. In addition, the variable r
represents a possible loss of reputation and η ∈ {0, 1}. Notice that, for simplicity, r
is an additional monetary loss a woman faces if being detected and it is, therefore,
part of her expenditures.
    A graphical illustration often improves intuition. Here, women have to decide
how much they are willing to risk and how many hours commercial sex are sup-
plied. In general, we look for the interior solution of the women’s maximization
problem – the point where the indifference curve and the budget constraint are
tangential to each other.
    Indifference curves show preferences of consumers and different sets of com-
modities realizing the same utility level. They are monotone – implying that more
is better – and have a decreasing slope.109 A positive slope would indicate that the
consumer prefers a less desirable bundle of goods, which is ruled out by assump-
tion. Indifference curves display a situation in which saturation has not yet been
reached. Furthermore, we assume that averages, meaning a combination of goods,
is always preferred to consuming only one good. This characteristic implies a con-
vex shape of the indifference curve. The slope of the indifference curve is given by
the marginal rate of substitution (MRS) – the rate for which a consumer is willing
to trade one unit of either good for one unit of the other good. When there is un-
certainty, the total expected utility is the absolute value of the marginal utilities in
both states multiplied by the probabilities. We ﬁnd the MRS by looking at the total
differential of the expected utility. For our problem here, the MRS is given by

                                   dCe    1 − ρ U  (Cn )
                                       =−                 .                                (3.62)
                                   dCn      ρ U  (Ce )

We are looking at a diagram with Ce at the ordinate and Cn at the abscissa. With
the slope of the budget line and the slope of the indifference curve given, we must
determine at which exact point on the diagram the agent consumes. At the opti-
mum, the indifference curve and the budget constraint cannot cross one another. In
most cases,110 the optimum is found at the point where the two lines are tangential.

109 Further explanations are, for example, given in Varian (2001, pp. 31), Breyer (2005, pp. 115),

Corneo (2003, pp. 115), Henderson and Quandt (1983, pp. 5), as well as in Pindyck and Rubinfeld
(2003, pp. 103, 221). The following discussion is mainly based on these publications.
110 Assuming an interior solution – what we did – even allows us to state that this is true for all

cases.
96                                                                   3 A Meta-Model

We now know that at the optimum the MRS must be equal to the relative prices.
Figure 3.5 shows two possible indifference curves i and ii with different slopes.
From equation 3.62, we immediately see that a change in the slope can be induced
by a change in the probability of getting caught ρ. With an increasing ρ, 1−ρ   ρ is
decreasing and the slope of the indifference curve also decreases. Point A marks
the efﬁcient combination of participating in the market for commercial sex – i.e.
the risky profession – and on the regular labor market – i.e. the secure profession.
The closer the point of consumption moves towards the abscissa – for example
to point B – the less prostitution will be supplied and the more other work. With
a higher probability of getting caught, participation in the market for commercial
sex becomes less attractive. How fast and intense this movement is depends on
the individual’s attitude towards risk. The decreasing slope then indicates that the
agent supplies more hours of work in the secure alternative. For an agent who does
not want to supply any amount of commercial sex, the indifference curve would
be as steep as the budget constraint and the only intersection point is then given on
the abscissa.

Now, equation 3.66 can be described by a budget line as shown in Figure 3.6. The

The demand curves are known to be the Marshallian demand functions and show

should be clear.

Our core interest is to analyze changes of the work women supply on the market for

we need to look at the differentials with respect to work in the commercial sex

this also allows us to make a statement on the decision to work in the regular

    So far, the probability of getting caught has not changed. If we assume an in-
crease in ρ, we immediately see that the ﬁrst term gains inﬂuence, whereas the
second term is now lower. In other words: With a higher probability of getting
caught, the negative term in equation 3.75 becomes more inﬂuential. A higher
probability of getting caught seems to negatively inﬂuence the supply of work in
the commercial sex sector. Of course, the opposite is true for the supply of work
on the regular labor market as the time constraint needs to hold in any case.
    It is interesting to note that both models we have looked at – although only
the latter models uncertainty explicitly – lead to the same outcome: Given that an
alternative labor market is present, a higher probability of getting caught selling
sexual services decreases the supply of working hours each woman is willing to
offer on this market. As a representative agent was assumed, this reaction should
be true for all women and therefore decrease the total supply on the commercial
sex market. Note also that in the Sandmo (1981) case the variable χ represents a
penalty tax if being caught. From equation 3.75 , we see that this χ even leads to a
more negative ﬁrst term and thus, the negative effect on the supply should be even
stronger. However, as χ is modeled as a penalty tax when being caught, the model
loses part of its validity for most recent years in Germany: German jurisdiction
does not punish legal prostitution anymore and in 2002 equalized the profession
to all other professions. Nevertheless, with the large variance of laws in Germany,
both models have a right to exist and both lead to reasonable (and similar) results.
How they can be applied to the actual history of prostitution is discussed in Chapter
4.



111 As we have not allowed for savings, the disposable income is equivalent to consumption.
3.3 Summary of Chapter 3                                                         101

3.3 Summary of Chapter 3

In this chapter, we developed two different models explaining the work decision
of women. We assumed that women have the choice between two kinds of work:
A risky profession – prostitution – and a secure alternative on the regular labor
market. An interior solution was assumed, which ensured that women always work
in both sectors.
    The ﬁrst model was built up on the standard approach of intertemporal budget
constraints. Accordingly, women did not only decide on work and consumption
today, but also took the future into account. We found that with a higher probability
of getting caught, the overall effect was negative. Women consume less, but also
offer more work on the regular labor market. With a binding time constraint, work
hours offered on the market for commercial sex declined.
    The second model was developed by Sandmo in 1981 to describe and discuss
the behavior of tax evaders. The model was adopted and modiﬁed for our research
question. Instead of only looking at budget constraints, we maximized a general
von Neumann-Morgenstern utility function, which causes uncertainty to enter the
women’s decisions. Here as well, we found that a higher probability of getting
caught decreased the supply of commercial sex.
    Although both models were structured very differently, they both showed the
same relationship. Looking at the history of prostitution, we should ﬁnd a lower
supply of commercial sex in these periods in which the probability of getting
caught relatively increased. Whether this is true is the central question of Chap-
ter 4.
Chapter 4
An Application of the Meta-Model




In the previous chapter, two different models were developed and discussed: One
based on rather simple intertemporal budget constraints and the other adapted from
Sandmo (1981) and modiﬁed for our research question. In the following, we apply
both models to the past, present, and – in Chapter 5 – future of the market for com-
mercial sex. Here, the variables assumed to have the biggest inﬂuence on the mar-
ket for prostitution services are discussed. We focus on explaining how the supply
of commercial sex has changed over time. In Chapter 3, especially from equations
3.45, 3.48, as well as 3.75, we found that the probability of getting caught has a
major inﬂuence on the amount of commercial sex supplied. The probability of get-
ting caught is directly inﬂuenced by society and jurisdiction: A permitted action is
less likely to be supervised by the police and curious neighbors. Ergo, we analyze
the development of the variable ρ in Section 4.5 in detail. At the very beginning
of Chapter 3, in equation 3.3, we assumed that there is a close connection between
the probability of getting caught and the loss of reputation. A different jurisdiction
can be interpreted as an indicator for changes in the attitude of society towards a
certain subject. This attitude, in turn, directly inﬂuences the stigmatization of pros-
titution, which has not vanished over time, but certainly ﬂuctuated, and with that
a possible loss of reputation. Thus, the possible loss of reputation is analyzed in
Section 4.4. We assume that changes in both variables are inﬂuenced by changes in
the legislation, taking into account that for decades, the legislator tried to inﬂuence
the situation on the market for commercial sex by enacting laws. Consequently,
the strictness of laws has changed frequently and shall be analyzed in Section 4.3.
Finally, for economists, the price always plays a central role and is closely related
to supply and demand on a market. This is why the price is discussed in Section
4.2. These four variables should, according to theory, inﬂuence the supply of com-
mercial sex and they are, in the following, analyzed one after the other starting
with the variable we intend to explain: the supply in Section 4.1.

Eva-Maria Heberer, Prostitution, DOI 10.1007/978-3-658-04496-1_4,
© Springer Fachmedien Wiesbaden 2014
104                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

    Of course, there are only few reliable data available for a market like the one
for commercial sex. Firstly, this is because it was immoral until the year 2002.
Shadow markets are never well documented as it is one of their characteristics
to work without governmental intervention. Secondly, the moral stigmatization of
prostitutes led to a much lower willingness of prostitutes to reveal their profession
(SOFFI K., 2005b, pp. 200). Even with available data however, it is questionable
whether one can rely on the numbers as the incentive to report wrong earnings
are just too high (Kavemann, 2009b, p. 97). Accordingly, we should be aware that
a data-based analysis is difﬁcult when talking about the market for prostitution
services.
    The second chapter reviewed and discussed the history of prostitution. We ex-
plained how and why changes in the legislative process were promoted and re-
alized in Germany. To simplify the analysis, we assume that the epochs deﬁned
in Chapter 2 are homogeneous within each other and only different between one
another. Hartmann (2006, p. 276) states that the most inﬂuential laws concern-
ing prostitution were the Lex Heinze in 1900, the GeschlKrG in 1927, as well as
the drop of the illegality of prostitution in 1974. Taking the recent developments
into account, the ProstG should be added to this list. In addition, Hartmann (2006)
does not discuss the GDR and its jurisdiction which also saw major innovations,
especially with the new StGB in 1968 and the fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989.
Finally, the Nazi regime calls for an independent analysis. With these changes in
legislation, the epochs seem to be deﬁned naturally. However, discussions on pros-
titution were carried out almost constantly throughout the period of analysis, but
these did not lead to major changes in jurisdiction – in fact, besides the aforemen-
tioned reforms, other changes in laws concerning prostitution were seldom and,
if enacted, only of low impact. Assuming a very close connection between laws
and their guiding inﬂuence on society in general, it seems reasonable to assume
that society’s standpoint did not change substantially within the epochs. We have
stated before that all of our variables of interest are especially inﬂuenced by so-
ciety and jurisdiction and their variance during each epoch is, accordingly, rather
small. We are aware that certain and slow developments cannot be detected with
this approach, but it seems the most promising from all possible procedures.
    As a starting point for analyzing the ﬂuctuations of our ﬁve variables, we dis-
cuss the means of measurement for each of them individually and suggest one in-
dicator to evaluate their behavior. To see whether this instrument is suitable for the
actual history of prostitution is essential as each variable is debatable and never
straightforwardly measured. As strict statements on the intensity are difﬁcult to
make, we order these variables on an ordinal scale indicating whether their inten-
sity increased or decreased compared to the other epochs. Note that the epochs we
are analyzing are exactly those we have deﬁned in Chapter 2 and we assess them
chronologically. In a last step, we try to show that with the order of magnitude
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                                 105

determined by measuring and discussing the history of prostitution as well as ob-
serving the models derived, we are able to predict whether the number of active
prostitutes increased or decreased for certain periods of time.112



4.1 Changes in the Supply

Being the variable of central interest, it seems reasonable to start the analysis by
looking at changes in the supply of commercial sex. All of the following variables
are assumed to have an inﬂuence on the supply – either directly or indirectly. Both
models we developed in Chapter 3 predict a similar behavior: A higher probability
of getting caught leads to a lower supply of commercial sex. As a matter of fact,
the following section seems to be crucial for our discussion. We have discussed
that ﬁnding reliable data is difﬁcult for a market like the one for commercial sex.
This could not be more true for the supply variable. However, we ﬁnd statements
for each epoch in the following and measure it relative to the total size of the
population in the respective area.



4.1.1 Means of Measurement

Women working in prostitution always had very little incentive to declare what
they do for a living. During the last decades, sex workers had to live with a double
standard of being denied the right to choose their profession on the one hand, but
being obliged to pay taxes on the other. The violation of moral principles was not
diminished until 2002 and, even today, women have to live with a moral stigma
from society. Prostitution is not a profession like any other. If it were, women
would have a chance to live a normal life after work. In Germany – as in most
other countries – you do not work as a prostitute you are one (see, e.g., Madonna
e.V. (2013)). What you are is more than what you do for a living and not left after
leaving work.
   This all makes it difﬁcult to ﬁnd reliable data on how many women work in
prostitution. Even with the ProstG valid, the majority of women work without the
knowledge of relatives and friends and therefore do not register for any authority.
In addition, the years of division between East and West Germany leave us with
two individual countries that are more or less incomparable. For this reason, Berlin

112 The reader should be aware that we can – due to reasons discussed in Chapter 3 – only make

statements on the behavior of the supply side of the market.
106                                                4 An Application of the Meta-Model

is a good option to analyze as both states were present in one city. The misfortune
lies, as often, in the lack of data. Prostitution was, in general, allowed in Germany
after 1974 and data on most variables are only available for states that implemented
a restricted area – Berlin, however, never had such an area. To our best knowledge,
no ofﬁcial data that we could work with is available for Berlin, such as the number
of prostitutes, number of arrests, or anything comparable. Left with the decision to
either give up on measuring the variables or to exclude one epoch – the GDR. We
decided for the second option and chose to look at Hamburg.
    Hamburg is the second largest city in Germany and located in the north close
to the sea. Due to the harbor, prostitution was always very present in the city and
nowadays the red light district is an area where many bars and clubs co-exist with
brothels and massage parlours that offer sexual services. During the 1990s, an
estimated 6,000 active prostitutes worked in the city (Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 63),
whereas the media reports there are 2,250 women selling commercial sex today
(Andrick, 2012). The police in Hamburg assumes that the market for commercial
sex is almost equally separated in three different sectors: apartment prostitution,
prostitution in brothels, and on the street (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 64). The market
for commercial sex in Hamburg is deﬁnitely one of the largest in Germany.
    Alfred Urban did much research on the topic of prostitution in Hamburg. Only
one of his books Urban (1927) is easily available. The other three parts (Urban
(1973a), Urban (1973b), and Urban (1973c)) were never published and the only de-
tectable copy is in the library of the Northern German broadcasting (Norddeutscher
Rundfunk, NDR). Fortunately, we were able to take a look at these manuscripts.
In his works, Urban describes the market for commercial sex in Hamburg with
a main focus on the statistics. Besides other factors, he states how many women
were registered in Hamburg as prostitutes for the years until 1972. Until the late
1970s, women working in the commercial sex sector in Hamburg had to register
and attend regular medical exams to test for infections (Freund-Widder, 2003, p.
283). After 1978, registration was voluntary. Freund-Widder (2003, p. 285) states
that due to this liberalization, registrations declined by almost 40% during the fol-
lowing ﬁve years. Unfortunately, Freund-Widder (2003) does not state a source
with data available for registered prostitutes after 1973. Neither was the request
for data at the public health department nor at the information center for sexu-
ally transmitted diseases in Hamburg succesful, and other newer data could not be
found. As a matter of fact, the data provided in Urban (1927), Urban (1973a), and
Urban (1973c) is the only set available.
    Of course, we can only make a statement on how the supply has changed if we
measure the amount of registered prostitutes relative to the population in Hamburg.
The Statistisches Amt für Hamburg und Schleswig-Holstein (2013b) provides an
almost complete time series for the population in Hamburg, which is shown in Fig-
ure 4.1. For the years 1918 and 1921, two data points are missing. The rest of the
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                       107

data can be found in Appendix A.1. The population in Hamburg increased steadily,
showing negative kinks only in the years 1917 and 1944. It was considered to look
only at the female population as we expect prostitution to be female dominated.
However, data on the female population as a complete time series is only available
after the Second World War.

                                       2,000,000

                                       1,800,000

                                       1,600,000

Data on registered prostitutes is given in Appendix A.2. Until 1869, women were
only registered if they applied for it. The application was, however, enforced by
the police (Urban, 1927, p. 30). After that year, the police registered women in-
dependently (Urban, 1927, p. 99). Having their name deleted from the list was, of
course, more difﬁcult: Women who were not born in Hamburg, which was true for
the majority (Urban, 1927, p. 39), for example, had to leave the city completely
if they wanted to avoid further controls (Urban, 1927, p. 33). Nevertheless, many
women changed their status frequently and numbers only reﬂect the amount of
registered women at one ﬁxed date (Urban, 1927, p. 35) and not the growth rates.
For the years until the turn of the century, the policies frequently changed: During
some years, women were only registered if they were sentenced for prostitution;
during others, only women who were already working quite some time could reg-
ister (Urban, 1927, p. 99). Since 1902, women were sentenced for prostitution for
following an unmoral lifestyle or testing positive for a venereal disease (Urban,
1927, p. 99). Surprisingly, the number of registered women was relatively stable
for almost 100 years (Urban, 1973a, p. 470), which becomes obvious when look-
ing at the number of women in brothels: In 1935, 350 offered their services in
one of the houses (Urban, 1973a, p. 399). By 1941, this number increased to 428
108                                                                                          4 An Application of the Meta-Model

(Urban, 1973a, p. 406) – a level it kept until 1958. After the Second World War,
Urban (1973a, p. 470) states that the amount of women working in brothels was
stable at around 400. Finally, between 1958 and 1967, the level was only 250, but
again without many variations for the next nine years (Urban, 1973a, p. 567).

Fig. 4.2: Absolute Number of Registered Women and per 1,000 Inhabitants in

In the left panel of Figure 4.2, we see the development of the absolute number of
registered women in Hamburg between 1845 and 1972. The years in which data
is missing have been deleted to avoid jumps in the graph. However, this was only
necessary for the years of the Second World War. Figure 4.2b shows the same vari-
able relative to 1,000 inhabitants in Hamburg. It is obvious to see that the absolute
number of registered prostitutes in Hamburg ﬂuctuates much more than the num-
ber per 1,000 inhabitants. Kinks occur especially in the years 1926 and 1932. Both
years are characterized by the fear of high infection rates. In 1927, the GeschlKrG
came into effect and, hence, registration rates declined. From then on, the health
authorities were in charge and not the police anymore. After the Second World
War, the number of registered women was comparatively small, which should not
be surprising as the authorities had other priorities than focusing on proper and
reliable statistics. Finally, since 1951, the number of registered prostitutes has in-
creased steadily.
    We see a different picture looking at the development of the number of regis-
tered prostitutes relative to 1,000 inhabitants in Hamburg. The ﬁrst epoch is es-
pecially characterized by intense ﬂuctuations, varying between 4.47 in 1857 and
0.88 in 1897. In the second epoch, the highest rate can be found in 1926 of 2.05
registered women per 1,000 inhabitants and the lowest in the following year with
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                        109

0.74. Again, the GeschlKrG is represented in the data. During the Nazi regime,
only a few women were registered, which is not a surprise: There was neither an
incentive to register voluntarily nor did the authorities supervise women without
taking further steps. The time series stops at the highest rate during the ﬁfth epoch
with 1.27 registered women per 1,000 inhabitants. It is reasonable to assume that
the rate dropped remarkably during the mid-1970s, but data are, unfortunately, not
available.

As we are interested in comparing one epoch against the other, to be able to given
an ordinal ordering of the supply of commercial sex, we look at the average number
of registered women in Hamburg per 1,000 inhabitants. This is given in Table 4.1.
We ﬁnd that during the ﬁrst epoch, the average is highest whereas the lowest has
been calculated for the time of Hitler’s dictatorship. Data are – to our best knowl-
edge – not available for the GDR. Korzilius (2005, p. 444) states that the police in
Berlin knew of 150 to 180 active prostitutes. It is questionable whether this data is
comparable to the number of registered women in Hamburg. However, 1,089,874
inhabitants lived in Berlin during the same year (Staatliche Zentralverwaltung für
Statistik, 1974). Accordingly, 0.17 women were registered as prostitutes per 1,000
inhabitants. Although not completely comparable, we still use this number in Table
4.1 as well as in Figure 4.3, which illustrates our ﬁndings.

period for the western part of Germany, is indicated by V . The period between
the reuniﬁcation and the ProstG has the index V I and the years since the ProstG
are indicated by V II.113 Taking the registered women per 1,000 inhabitants as a
measure, we ﬁnd the following ordinal ordering:

The question remains whether looking at the number of registered prostitutes is
an appropriate proxy to measure the supply on the market for commercial sex.
In a discussion, some members of the Reichstag claimed that not more than 10%
of women who offered services were in fact registered during the outgoing 19th
century (Reichstag, 1893, p. 931). Heinz-Trossen (1993, pp. 117) also claims that
the estimated number of unregistered prostitutes in the commercial sex sector is
huge for later years. In fact, it might give us more insight into the real conditions
of the market if we consider the number of estimated prostitutes instead of the
number of registered women. Time series are, for obvious reasons, not available
for this variable. However, we ﬁnd and discuss statements given in the literature in
Section 4.1.2. By the end of this section, we are able to see whether equation 4.1
provides an adequate ordering.



4.1.2 Looking Back

Finding statements on the estimated amount of prostitution is difﬁcult; ﬁnding re-
liable statements is close to impossible. Reviewing such a long and diverse history
of prostitution requires reading a large amount of literature on the topic – one of
the major problems is, however, that not all sources provide reliable information.
The literature discussed includes – just to name a few – summarizing works, expe-
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                       111

rience reports, reports from contemporary witnesses, publications written by lobby
groups as well as the government, theoretical approaches, statistical reports, and
history books. Taking into account the pure amount of literature published on the
topic, it is surprising how little data on the number of women offering commercial
sex is available as the vast majority quote the same sources over and over again.
Still, we were able to ﬁnd at least one statement on an appropriate estimation of
the number of women working in prostitution for each epoch.
    After brothels were closed in 1846, not even the bad salary prevented an exten-
sion of the supply. Many thought the morals of society were on the decline and
the number of prostitutes rose rapidly (Hügel, 1865, pp. 150). There is no doubt
that only a small fraction of active prostitutes was ofﬁcially registered (Evans,
1976, p. 114) and that street prostitution increased rapidly after closing of broth-
els (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 48). However, 15,000 prostitutes in Berlin, as Röhrmann
(1846, p. 29) suggests, seems to be an overestimate, although this number is again
stated in Schulte (1994, p. 20) for later years. After the reallowance in 1858, 24
brothels opened in Berlin; at the same time, Hamburg had already 124 brothels
(Hügel, 1865, p. 156). Not only did the number of prostitutes rise, but also the
amount of women who were supervised by medical services in Berlin. Whereas,
for example, 995 women were supervised at the beginning of 1867, this number
rose to 1,447 in December (Lacroix and Helbing, 1995, p. 102). Within the same
year, the number of women suspected to work as a prostitute rose from 10,860 to
12,491 (Lacroix and Helbing, 1995, p. 102). In general, it seems that the numbers
for ofﬁcially registered women in Berlin rapidly increased during the end of the
19th century: 1886: 3,006; 1887: 3,067; 1888: 3,392; 1889: 3,703; 1890: 4,039
(Lacroix and Helbing, 1995, p. 115). During the aforementioned discussion in the
Reichstag, a comparably high number of 50,000 prostitutes for Berlin was esti-
mated as only 10% of all prostitutes were expected to be registered at the turn of
the century (Reichstag, 1893, p. 931). At the same time, 180,000 prostitutes were
estimated to work in Germany (see, e.g., Lacroix and Helbing (1995, pp. 115) and
Evans (1976, p. 108)). In summary, the trend found in the end of Section 4.1.1
can be conﬁrmed: The turn of the century seems to be an era characterized by a
high number of women working in the commercial sex sector. Taking 180,000 as
a benchmark and comparing it to a population of 40,997,000 for the year 1871
(Statistisches Bundesamt, 2007) – out of 1,000 inhabitants in Germany, 4.39 were
working in prostitution during that epoch.
    In 1900, 939 women were registered as prostitutes in Hamburg (Urban, 1927,
p. 98) and only three years later, we ﬁnd 3,709 registered women in Berlin (cited
from Schulte (1994, p. 20)). Of course, Hamburg and Berlin were probably the
areas with the highest amount of commercial sex sold, but the incentive to regis-
ter voluntarily did not increase compared to the ﬁrst epoch. Ergo, one should bear
in mind that many women still worked unregistered. Due to worse living condi-
112                                                      4 An Application of the Meta-Model

tions during the First World War, the number of part time prostitutes increased
(Sauerteig, 1999, pp. 391) and in total, 330,000 prostitutes were estimated for the
war years (Evans, 1976, p. 108). The number did not decline to its pre-war level af-
ter 1918 and instead remained high (Hartmann, 2006, pp. 282). In general, one has
to admit that the number of medically supervised women steadily increased un-
til 1929 (cited from Sauerteig (1999, p. 412)). Especially during and after World
War I, the number of active prostitutes seems to be higher than it was before.
Sauerteig (1999, p. 58) states that between 100,000 and 200,000 prostitutes could
be estimated for the beginning of the new century. At the same time, the German
population was around 56,046,000 people (Statistisches Bundesamt, 2007), lead-
ing to 3.57 women working in the commercial sex sector per 1,000 inhabitants.
Compared to the ﬁrst epoch, the share decreased – a relationship we also saw in
equation 4.1.
    To ﬁnd reliable numbers for the amount of active prostitutes during the Nazi
regime is even more difﬁcult. There was no incentive at all for prostitutes to state
their true profession. During the early 1930s, people’s earnings were low, the un-
employment rate was high, and the supply of prostitution increased (Sauerteig,
1999, pp. 413). How many of them were sent to the concentration camps remains
questionable. Up to 1943, 110,000 people who were not Jewish were sent into one
of the concentration camps. From these, two thirds were deported due to asocial
behavior (Bock, 1986, p. 364).114 A fellow prisoner stated that between 80 and 90
women in his camp were for offering prostitution services (Paul, 1994, p. 90) and
Paul (1994, p. 135) estimates that there were 500 brothels for the Wehrmacht, 60
for forced laborers, and nine within concentration camps. She states that one can
assume that approximately 34,000 women were forced into prostitution during the
Nazi regime (Paul, 1994, p. 135). Although the number of street prostitution de-
clined to a level close to zero, forced prostitution was common. As other data on
the estimated amount of prostitution during Hitler’s dictatorship is not available,
we use the information that 150 women worked in a licensed brothel in Cologne
during 1934 and, in total, 1,600 were supervised by the police (Spiethoff, 1934, p.
2). In the same year, 66,409,000 people lived in Germany (Statistisches Bundes-
amt, 2007) and in 1939, 768,293 were listed for the city of Cologne (Stadt Köln,
2011). With these numbers, we ﬁnd that 2.08 out of 1,000 inhabitants in Cologne
offered services during the Nazi regime.
    Compared to the West, the supply was very low within the GDR; one estimates
that only 3,000 prostitutes were professional sex workers (Falck, 1998, p. 19). In
Berlin – one of only two cities with street prostitution (the other one being Leipzig)
– the police knew of only 150 to 180 active women (Korzilius, 2005, p. 444). With

114 One must keep in mind, however, that asocial behavior included much more than prostitution

only.
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                          113

the building of the Berlin Wall, this number declined even further as many women
left for West Germany (Falck, 1998, pp. 76). However, the market for commercial
sex shrank not only in the eastern part, but also in the western part of the city: Many
sex workers feared that demand would vanish and decided to move to other parts of
the FRG (Falck, 1998, pp. 76). The Statistisches Bundesamt (2007, p. 35) declares
that 16,740,000 people lived in the GDR in 1980. Thus, out of 1,000 inhabitants,
0.18 are estimated to work in the commercial sex sector. Even compared to the
Nazi regime, this number is the lowest found so far. In Section 4.1.1, an equivalent
trend could be found.
    Interestingly, the supply of commercial sex was quite stable for almost 100
years in West Germany (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, p. 118). Summarizing ﬁndings from
previous studies, Stallberg (1988, p. 11) assumes that around 200,000 women
working in the commercial sex sector is a realistic estimation for the late 1980s.
Also unchanged was the low willingness of women to register as a prostitute
and Stallberg (1988, p. 11), therefore, assumes that the number of approximately
60,000 registered women does not reﬂect the reality of West Germany during the
1970s and 1980s. Compared to the GDR as well as to the years of the Nazi regime,
the number of active prostitutes was much higher in the western part of the coun-
try. Taking 200,000 estimated prostitutes as a benchmark and relating that to the
German population of 61,715,103 in 1988 (Statistisches Bundesamt, 2007), we
ﬁnd that per 1,000 inhabitants 3.24 offered commercial sex in the FRG during the
country’s division. The higher population in the late 1980s accounts for this – com-
pared to the ﬁrst and second epoch – lower relationship. This is again in line with
our previous ﬁndings.
    The number of active prostitutes varied across Germany. In Hamburg, esti-
mates went up to 10,000 working in the commercial sex sector (Leopold et al.,
1994, p. 63); for Berlin one assumed up to 5,000 prostitutes and 50 brothels
(Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 85). Dortmund had around 1,000 prostitutes and 250
clubs (Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 119). A similar number was estimated for Frank-
furt/Main (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 139) and Stuttgart (Leopold et al., 1994, p.
166). In Munich, from the 1,200 prostitutes 20% worked in a brothel and 8% in
apartments. The rest mainly worked on the streets (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 189).
Rostock, Dresden, and Leipzig only had around 100 prostitutes each (Leopold
et al., 1994, pp. 204, 220, 239). In general, one assumes that there was an under-
supply in the eastern part and an oversupply in the West (Leopold et al., 1994,
p. 256). During the 1990s, 250 million services were sold per year demanded by
1.2 million men every day (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 5). In general, two thirds of all
men demanded services from the commercial sex sector (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 5).
Carried out during the ﬁrst years after the fall of the Berlin Wall, the publication
by Leopold et al. (1994) gives great insight into the market for commercial sex in
Germany. The authors are probably right to state that a reliable estimation for Ger-
114                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

many as a whole is almost impossible with so little data available (Leopold et al.,
1994, p. 255). The data presented in Section 4.1.1 does not provide any numbers
for registered women in Hamburg for the reunited country either. It is true, how-
ever, that the supply in the former GDR was comparatively small (Leopold et al.,
1994, p. 256) and Heinz-Trossen (1993, p. 118) states that 212,000 active pros-
titutes seems to be a proper estimation for the western part of Germany. Taking
these 212,000 women as a benchmark and taking into account that the German
population was 80,974,632 in 1992 (Statistisches Bundesamt, 2007), out of 1,000
inhabitants, 2.62 are estimated to work in the commercial sex sector shortly after
the fall of the Berlin Wall.115

After the ProstG came into effect, the market for commercial sex changed from a
half-legal environment (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 1) into a market offering improved
working conditions for women. The number of women working in prostitution
does not seem to have changed much. Most authors still state that around 200,000
active prostitutes is the best estimation possible for the recent years (see, e.g., Do-
mentat (2003, p. 45) and SOFFI K. (2005a, p. 239)). Hamburg is still the city with
the largest commercial sex sector with 2,250 prostitutes working there (Andrick,
2012). Women working in Hamburg do not fall victim to forced medical tests any-
more and are not obliged to register for their profession. Accordingly, the data on
registered women does not provide any information for this seventh epoch. Taking
the often stated number of 200,000 estimated prostitutes in Germany as a bench-


115 Even adding the 3,000 active prostitutes in the GDR (Falck, 1998, p. 19) to that number, does

not change the position of NV I within the ordering. Per 1,000 inhabitants, we then have 2.66
women estimated to have worked in prostitution.
116 Inhabitants for the year 1939.
4.1 Changes in the Supply                                                        115

mark and knowing that in 2003, 82,531,671 people lived in Germany (Statistisches
Bundesamt, 2007), of 1,000 inhabitants 2.42 were working in prostitution.
   To summarize, we do not ﬁnd many ﬂuctuations in the amount of women offer-
ing commercial sex during the last 167 years. However, relative to the number of
people living in the country, the share did vary. In Section 4.1.1, we were only able
to order ﬁve out of seven parameters as data on registered women in Hamburg is
not available for the last two epochs. Surprisingly, we ﬁnd the same ordering for
these ﬁve parameters when looking at the number of estimated instead of registered
women. Table 4.2 summarizes the ﬁndings of this section stating the year, the esti-
mated number of women working in the commercial sex sector, the population at
the same time, and the number of prostitutes per 1,000 inhabitants.
   As equation 4.1 seems to give an appropriate ordering of the supply of com-
mercial sex in Germany, we keep the ranks as suggested. Completing equation
4.1 by the remaining two parameters NV I and NV II , we ﬁnd the following ordinal
equation:
                   N I > N II > NV > NV I > NV II > N III > N IV .              (4.2)
The same relationship is shown again graphically in ﬁgure 4.4. Per 1,000 inhabi-
tants, the supply of commercial sex was largest during the ﬁrst epoch – until the
year 1899 – and lowest during the GDR. The Nazi regime also accounts for lit-
tle supply. In West Germany, the supply seems to have steadily decreased since
the Second World War. As data availability and quality is limited, these numbers
should be interpreted with caution. In the next sections, we keep the structure sug-
gested here and look at the other variables we have previously deﬁned.
