4.2 Changes in the Price

Prices assign a value for the good we buy. Prices for a service – most commonly
called a wage – assign a certain value to the work of the respective employee.
For prostitution, many assume that it is well paid compared to a woman’s skills
(see, e.g., Edlund and Korn (2002, p. 182)) and her small time exposure. In the
following, we discuss how prices for prostitution have changed over the last 167
years. With these ﬁndings, we are then able to compare the previously deﬁned
epochs in terms of earning possibilities within the commercial sex sector.



4.2.1 Means of Measurement

The myth of the huge earning possibilities of prostitution is probably responsible
for a major interest in the prices for sexual services. Not everybody has gained
enough insight into the market for commercial sex to know how much – or how
little – women earn working in prostitution. It has been argued before that it should
be expected that women receive some kind of compensation when offering sexual
services as they bear a high risk of being detected, lose some kind of reputation,
and are performing a job most women refuse to do.
    The wages a sex worker earns is one of her best-kept secrets. The incentives
to report false earnings are huge and work in both directions. Kavemann (2009b,
p. 97) explains that the stigmatization of prostitution can induce an overstatement:
Often, the high earnings are an argument in favor of prostitution – not only for so-
ciety but also for oneself. On the other hand, questions about earnings are mostly
asked by ofﬁcial authorities and even if surveys are carried out anonymously,
women working in prostitution have learned to mistrust any kind of authority. The
fear of high tax payments might especially lead to an understatement of earnings
(Kavemann, 2009b, p. 97). Considering the aforementioned incentives, we need to
be aware that the information given here cannot be fully trusted.
    This situation is aggravated by the fact that earnings vary between sectors, es-
pecially in the market for commercial sex.117 Basically, one has to assume three
different markets exist: In the escort sector, women earn comparatively high wages
and the choice of working there is not made due to a lack of alternatives. Often, the
women are highly educated (see, e.g., Edlund and Korn, 2002, p. 187), have very
good manners, and are more than just good looking. During the 1990s, the market
for commercial sex in Dresden was characterized by a majority of women working

117 See, e.g., Ahlburg and Jensen (1998, p. 151), who ﬁnd that the price of sex depends on the

location.
4.2 Changes in the Price                                                           117

as escorts. Earnings up to 300 DM per service were possible there (Leopold et al.,
1994, p. 240). In the brothel sector, earnings are lower and the working conditions
vary to a large extent. Often, standards are not given,118 but since the ProstG, de-
mands to intervene and create a legal basis have occurred (SOFFI K., 2005b, pp.
88). However, there certainly are brothels offering a good working environment
(see, e.g., Winter (2009, p. 227)) and brothels have, compared to the street sec-
tor, one major advantage: They offer some privacy for the women and customers
and therefore, working indoors seems to be much more convenient. For this con-
venience, women often have to pay high rents (see, e.g., Leopold et al. (1994,
pp. 144)) and their proﬁts are, after deducting costs, lower than one might expect
(Leopold et al., 1994, p. 145). Sadly, street prostitution is often driven by drug
addiction, poverty, and debt (see, e.g., Kavemann and Rabe (2007, p. 16)). For the
women, this creates a hopeless situation and reduces their bargaining power. In
addition, Gertler and Shah (2009, p. 15) ﬁnd that street-walkers "tend to be older,
less educated, and less attractive than brothel sex workers". Due to these reasons,
earnings on the street are lower than in the brothel or escort sector. Leipzig and
Berlin were the only two cities with street prostitution in the GDR. Due to that,
the street sector developed faster there than in other eastern cities after the fall of
the Berlin Wall and women working in Leipzig could demand higher prices than
other street-walkers in Germany; earnings up to 100 DM per service were possible
in the 1990s (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 223).
    Of course, differences in prices are not only driven by sector speciﬁcs but
also by other matters. The economy of the market region plays a major role. In
Stuttgart, the price for commercial sex was the highest in Germany during the
1990s (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 169), which is most likely caused by the compara-
tively high earnings realized in the South of Germany (see, e.g., ifo Institut (2012)).
Assuming a free market, the price for a good will be at the exact point where the
quantity supplied and demanded are the same (see, e.g., Pindyck and Rubinfeld
(2003, p. 53)) and a situation of market clearing has been reached. These basic
characteristics are, of course, also true for the market of commercial sex. Ergo, a
price always represents how demand and supply interact with each other, whether
either side has some market power, and if the market has reached an equilibrium.
    Talking about price differences over such a long period of time always leaves
us with the problem of inﬂation and the consequence of varying values of money.
People living and working in the 1890s earned much less than people today. To
make the statements about prices comparable, we measure the wage a prostitute
receives per service relative to the price of one kg of rye bread.119 For the years
until 1959, Hoffmann (1965) observes the DM prices for one kg of rye bread. Af-

118 For a discussion on this, see, e.g., SOFFI K. (2005b, pp. 72).
119 All data is given in Appendix A.3.
118                                                                                   4 An Application of the Meta-Model

ter that, however, we use data provided by the Statistisches Bundesamt (2004),
which gives average prices for goods used to calculate the consumer price index.
Unfortunately, these are not fully comparable to Hoffmann (1965).120 After 2002,
the consumer price index, given by the Statistisches Bundesamt (2013c), was used.
For the GDR, the documentation is much easier as the prices for many goods were
state-dictated since 1951.121

                                                   5.00
                   Price for One kg of Rye Bread




                                                   4.00

                                                          Fig. 4.5: Price for One kg of Rye Bread


The black line displays the prices in Germany as a whole and for West Germany
during the years of separation. The gray line shows the situation in the GDR.
During the ﬁrst years of analysis, the price for bread was stable, ﬂuctuating only
slightly. Unfortunately, data for the period of the First World War and the following
years are missing. The same is true for the years between 1939 and 1950. After the
Second World War, the price for bread increased very quickly in West Germany.
The GDR prevented this development as prices for certain goods were dictated by
the government. Accordingly, the price for rye bread was stable at 0.52 Mark-East
after 1951.
    Until now, we have not looked at the prices for prostitution services in Germany
at all. However, we have seen that complete time series on prices are not even

120 A further discussion on the comparability of the prices is given in Section 5.2.
121 Prices for the GDR are given in Mark-East as both currencies were, in fact, exchanged at a

rate 1:1 after the fall of the Berlin Wall. It should be kept in mind that this does not reﬂect reality
for the owners of DM during the GDR – they realized a much better exchange rate (cited from
Falck (1998, p. 147)).
4.2 Changes in the Price                                                                      119

available for a standard good like rye bread. Observations for prices valid on the
commercial sex sector, are even scarcer. Consequently, we only try to ﬁnd one
observation for each of the seven previously deﬁned epochs and measure it in
relation to the bread price in the same year. Different kinds of price observations
are possible – one could look at the monthly income of a prostitute, her earnings
per client, or the price of one service. We were only able to ﬁnd enough statements
for the last variable and even there we do not know what kind of service the price
reﬂects. Nevertheless, we are still able to give an ordinal ordering of the variable
p at the end of this section.



4.2.2 Looking Back

As before, we order the ﬁndings chronologically and start by looking at the ﬁrst
period: the years between 1846 and 1899. Finding statements about the price for
commercial sex is not easy, particularly for these years. Women working in the
lowest sector, street prostitution, especially had huge problems enforcing an ap-
propriate price during the ﬁrst years of analysis (Anonymous, 1870, p. 246) and
offered services as low as 50 Centimes in Paris (Jeannel, 1869, p. 130).122 In con-
trast, women in a higher segment in Paris earned between ﬁve and 10 Francs per
service (Jeannel, 1869, p. 117).123 For Germany, we ﬁnd a statement in Anony-
mous (1870, pp. 243, 245): Women working in the more expensive brothels in
Hamburg earned between three and ﬁve thaler, whereas the employees of the less
exclusive houses demanded between one and two thaler per service around the
year 1870. Taking three thaler as an estimation, we use the price given by the Sta-
tistisches Landesamt (1854, p. 135) of 0.10 thaler for one kg of rye bread.124 It
follows that in order to pay for one service on the commercial sex sector, one had
to give up 30 kg of bread in the ﬁrst period.
    For the time between the turn of the century and the seizure of power by the
Nazis, we ﬁnd different statements. Prostitutes working in the lower segment in
Berlin earned between three and 10 RM in 1907 (Ostwald, 1920, p. 20). Only

122 The different price segments have been discussed already in Section 4.2.1.
123 Taking ﬁve Francs as an estimation and using the relationship between thaler and Francs given

in Jeannel (1869, p. 185), we ﬁnd that around 1.95 thaler was the price of commercial sex in 1869
in Paris.
124 The annual abstract of statistics for Hamburg does not state a price for rye bread for that

year. The only source found is the Statistisches Landesamt (1854) stating at least a price for the
year 1853. In Stötzner (2002), we ﬁnd an explanation of the different symbols used. One thaler
accounted for 30 Groschen – in Hamburg, Schilling replaced the Groschen – and 12 Pfennig were
one Groschen.
120                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

twenty years later, in 1927, women working in the countryside earned between
1.50 and two RM (Bock, 1980, p. 77) – a much lower price for a sexual service.
A common phenomenon is that, due to higher earnings, prices in cities are higher
than in the countryside, which also seems to be true here (Bock, 1980, p. 77). As
the second statement seems to be rather too low, a price of 10 RM shall be assumed.
The Statistisches Amt der Stadt Berlin (1920) states that the average price of one
kg of rye bread was 0.31 RM in 1907. Accordingly, one had to give up 32 kg of
bread to pay for one service on the commercial sex market.
   The Nazis intended to provide prostitutes for every man (Timm, 2002, p. 252)
– accordingly, we ﬁnd complaints that prostitution services were comparatively
expensive in Berlin (Timm, 2002, p. 252). After this complaint, demands from
the authorities arose to open "brothels near all the large train stations to serve
soldiers exclusively during their temporary stays in Berlin" (Timm, 2002, pp. 252).
Boderach (1984) provides a collection of reports from Nazi members during the
regime. According to this publication, a woman earned between three and ﬁve
RM per client in 1943 (Boderach, 1984, p. 6070). As this was a year of war and,
accordingly, no information for the price of bread is available, the pre-war price
from 1938 of 0.30 RM was used (Statistisches Bundesamt, 1952). Using this price
of bread and the upper bound of the statement given in Boderach (1984, p. 6070),
the opportunity cost was almost 17 kg of bread during the third epoch.
   Prostitution was, in general, assumed to be very well paid both in the GDR and
in West Germany. The data for prices in the GDR, however, is very diverse and,
thus, a reliable statement is hard to make. The top salary observed was 5,000 Mark-
West per service (cited from Falck (1998, p. 116)), but other interviewed women
claimed to earn only 50 Mark-West per service (cited from Falck (1998, pp. 118)).
The Stasi assumed that women had around 1,000 Mark-East per month (cited from
Falck (1998, p. 121)). In general, men from West Germany had to pay higher
prices for the same services (Falck, 1998, p. 138), which led to demands of up to
300 Mark-West in Interhotels during the 1980s (cited from Falck (1998, p. 144)).
A good exchange rate made the payment of men from West Germany even more
interesting (cited from Falck (1998, p. 147)). During the years of the GDR, the
price of commercial sex was more or less stable (Falck, 1998, p. 19). However, a
decreasing supply of street prostitution within the last years led to a price of almost
100 Mark-East in Berlin in 1989 (cited from Falck (1998, p. 168)). One kg of bread
had a stable price of 0.52 Mark-East in the GDR (Staatliche Zentralverwaltung für
Statistik, 1989). Ergo, one had to give up an enormous amount of bread – 192 kg
– to buy one service on the commercial sex sector.
   It is not much easier to ﬁnd statements on how much women earned in West
Germany at the same time. During the 1980s, one assumed an annual turnover of
six to 10 billion DM for the whole industry (Kaiser, 1996, pp. 924) and the tax
ofﬁce estimated daily earnings of up to 2,000 DM per woman. Ten years later –
4.2 Changes in the Price                                                              121

during the 1990s – the annual turnover was assumed to have increased to 12.5
billion DM for the commercial sex sector (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 5). It seems that
the earnings for the majority of women increased over time, together with an in-
creasing self-conﬁdence of the entire generation of women. Die Grünen (1990, p.
9) state that women had to cover ﬁxed costs of approximately 200 DM per day in
1987 and needed four customers to earn this amount. Therefore, it seems reason-
able to assume a price of 50 DM per service. For 1987, we ﬁnd a price of 3.12 DM
per kg of bread (Statistisches Bundesamt, 1991). Hence, a little more than 16 kg
bread had to be given up to pay for one service on the commercial sex sector in
West Germany during the late 1980s.
    After the fall of the Berlin Wall, the price of commercial sex varied between
states. In Frankfurt/Main, women earned between 1,000 DM and 10,000 DM per
month (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 145) and in Stuttgart, the earnings were compar-
atively high with 100 DM to 150 DM per service (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 169).
In Leipzig, the supply was rather small, which led to prices of up to 100 DM
per service and earnings up to 15,000 DM per month (Leopold et al., 1994, p.
222). An escort dominated market occurred in Dresden and prices around 300 DM
were charged (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 240). Taking Hamburg as a benchmark, we
assume a price of 50 DM per service for the early years after the reuniﬁcation
(Leopold et al., 1994, p. 65). For the same time, we ﬁnd a price of 3.39 DM per
kg of bread (Statistisches Bundesamt, 1991), accounting for an opportunity cost of
almost 15 kg of bread per service.
    Data on prices for sexual services today have improved since women are more
willing to cooperate. It is still questionable, however, if they always state the truth
(Kavemann, 2009b, p. 97). The majority earns around 1,500 e per month, with
full-time workers earning a little bit more than part-timers (Kavemann, 2009b,
pp. 96). If working in a brothel, the women mostly have to share their proﬁts
with the owner, who commonly receives half of the revenue (Holznagel, 2009, p.
233). The Berlin tax ofﬁce estimates that a woman working in prostitution earns
around 150 e per day (Ernst-Pörksen, 2009, p. 247). It is estimated that the state
loses a total of two billion e of taxes per year from the commercial sex sector
(Bundesrechnungshof, 2003, p. 30). In St. Georg, a district in Hamburg where
many rather poorly paid prostitutes work, the common price for one hour of service
is around 30 e (Andrick, 2012). The price for bread was around 2.07 e in 2012
(GfK, 2013).125 Accordingly, we can assume that one has to give up a little more
than 14 kg of bread in order to pay for one service in St. Georg.
    Table 4.3 summarizes our ﬁndings and displays the year, price per service in
the commercial sex sector, price for bread, and the ratio between both indicators.

125 The GfK only reports average prices weighted by quantity and the information was given

during a telephone interview.

be very close together if we take into account that the lowest segment was taken
into consideration for the last epoch. It seems that, for many epochs, prostitutes
were able to realize almost stable prices in relation to one kg of bread.

4.3 Changes in the Law

It is commonly assumed that laws reﬂect both the will of a society as well as its
norms and its attitude towards morality. It seems, however, reasonable to assume an
interplay between society’s attitude and laws instead of a unidirectional inﬂuence
– society forms laws, but laws also inﬂuence the attitude of society. Ergo, looking
at how the jurisdiction changed over time gives us further insight into how society,
government, and the authorities in charge judged prostitution.



126 Price for the year 1853.
127 Price from the year 1938.

                                         192.31




                30.00    32.26
                                 16.67            16.03   14.75   14.49


                  I       II      III     IV       V       VI     VII



Fig. 4.6: The Price for Commercial Sex over the Years Relative to One kg of Rye
          Bread


4.3.1 Means of Measurement

Measuring changes in law does not seem to be problematic – one just has to look
at each change and see whether the law became more or less strict. Unfortunately,
it is never as easy as it seems. Firstly, it is often unclear what stricter means and
how one law can be evaluated against another. Let us take the GeschlKrG of 1927
as an example. Since that law came into effect, prostitution was in fact not illegal
anymore. However, women were obliged to attend regular health tests and, con-
sequently, it was easier for authorities to supervise the market (Sauerteig, 1999,
p. 360). How shall we judge whether the GeschlKrG or its ancestor was stricter?
It seems that too many different factors interact, which makes the analysis less
straightforward as originally expected. Furthermore, we try to make a statement
on how the laws changed between the deﬁned epochs; during most epochs, how-
ever, more than one change in legislation occurred and evaluating one variation
against the other seems even more difﬁcult.
    To measure the strictness of laws, a comparable measure needs to be deter-
mined. Strict laws often indicate a higher priority of authorities to regulate that
part of the shadow market. In the case of the commercial sex market, one could
observe the number of raids or members of the vice squad. For both variables, how-
ever, data are not available. Choosing a broader indicator is more promising. One
possibility is the number of arrests due to illegal prostitution. Due to the federal
124                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

structure of Germany, we chose to gather data for one state, as the laws concerning
prostitution vary between the states. As before, Hamburg was chosen.
    The years between 1807 and 1922 are statistically reviewed and discussed in
Urban (1927), but ofﬁcial statistics on arrests are missing until 1862. The author
argues that the number of women sent to seek medical service on the suspicion
of prostitution is a good approximation for arrests – a claim we agree with. Ac-
cordingly, the number of arrests between 1855 and 1862 are based on this as-
sumption.128 For the following years, Urban (1927) gives the number of ofﬁcially
arrested women, but information is still missing for some years. From 1922 un-
til 1971, time series are, unfortunately, not available, but Freund-Widder (2003)
at least provides some observations and additional information is given in Urban
(1973c).129 Since 1971, data are available on arrests made in Hamburg for illegal
prostitution as it is an offense according to §184e StGB and,130 therefore, cap-
tured in the Hamburg crime statistics. According to the Senat Hamburg (1980), it
is forbidden to sell commercial sex in Hamburg during certain times and in certain
areas, although indoor prostitution is tolerated (Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 64).131

Problems with the data arise as crimes due to the paragraph §184e StGB, which
are relevant for our question, are not documented individually, but include differ-
ent crimes.132 Consequently, the data given in Appendix A.4 should be treated
very carefully as the comparability of the observations is limited.133 Nevertheless,
it still seems to be a great starting point for measuring the strictness of laws con-
cerning the commercial sex sector. Talking about a topic like prostitution, we have
to deal with the lack of complete and comparable data. Despite, we think this is no
reason to refrain from the topic in general or to try to measure certain variables.
We suggest one starting point here and further research can hopefully contribute
to making the analysis even more reliable.

Even after the fall of the Berlin Wall, we do not ﬁnd a steady decrease, but rather a
ﬂuctuation, in the number of arrests. Although in 2012 551 people were arrested,
the level is much lower nowadays than during previous time periods.

however, that the average increased after the ProstG came into effect compared to
the years following the reuniﬁcation. The equalization of prostitution intended a
movement of the whole market out of the illegality. Accordingly, this number is
rather disappointing.

As it is the goal to make a statement about Germany as a whole and not about
Hamburg exclusively, we brieﬂy review German history in the following. After
that, we are able to comment on whether the number of arrests in Hamburg is an
acceptable proxy for the changes in law affecting the market for commercial sex
in Germany as a whole.



4.3.2 Looking Back

One of the ﬁrst inﬂuential reforms regarding prostitution laws was realized dur-
ing the 19th century and marks our starting point: Before 1846, prostitution was
allowed in Prussia as long as it took place indoors, which changed during the
same year (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 23). Brothels were closed down and forbidden
everywhere – the jurisdiction concerning prostitution became much stricter. The
legislation process, however, did not come to an end and demands from all sides
were smoldering in the background. Only ﬁve years later – in 1851 – the ban on
brothels was again lifted and, consequently, prostitutes were only punished if they
were caught not following police regulations. Although the laws do not seem to
be that radical during the turn of the century, no legal gray areas were left for
women working in prostitution (Hartmann, 2006, pp. 40) and reality was tough.
The police force was the sole power in charge and control mechanisms were not
present. A warrant, for example, was not necessary to send a potential prostitute to
a workhouse (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 24) and from 1852 the police could search a sus-
128                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

pect’s apartment during the night times (Gleß, 1999, p. 52). However, one should
note that the police’s goal was not to abolish prostitution, but rather to regulate it
(Jeannel, 1869, p. 189).
    Due to the Lex Heinze, discussions about the commercial sex sector were very
present at the turn of the century. The morals of society were legally already hurt
if prostitutes offered their services (Gleß, 1999, p. 80) and during World War I
the legislation became even stricter (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 394). Since 1917, peo-
ple demanded to close all brothels and to stop the regimentation of prostitution
in general (Reichstag, 1918, p. 1710). However, it took three more years – until
the 1920s – that this request was satisﬁed and the commercial sex sector was no
longer regulated by the state (Roos, 2002, p. 68). Probably the most urgent fear
of society and authorities was of sexually transmitted diseases. The tightening of
laws concerning the possible infection of others followed: Since 1918, one could
be arrested for spreading a venereal disease (Sauerteig, 2001, pp. 84). Finally, in
the late 1920s, brothels were closed down completely (Evans, 1976, p. 128) and
the infected were registered (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 343). This all came into effect
with the so-called GeschlKrG in 1927. Under this law, prostitutes no longer had to
register for their profession, but were still strictly supervised because one assumed
they had a higher risk of being infected by venereal diseases (Hartmann, 2006, p.
140). At the beginning of the new century, laws concerning prostitution seem to
have been much more strict. The fear of being infected with a disease was very
present within society and, consequently, prostitution was outlawed. Especially
during the First World War, the government had a major interest to lower the in-
fection rates and ban prostitution in general. However, one has to keep in mind that
the GeschlKrG led to one of the most radical changes in German jurisdiction con-
cerning prostitution (Hartmann, 2006, p. 276). The GeschlKrG was more or less
valid in Germany until 2002 (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, p. 62) and its inﬂuence cannot
be understated. Ergo, one can argue that with the validation of the GeschlKrG,
laws were less strict in the second epoch than during the ﬁrst epoch, although data
on arrests in Hamburg suggest the opposite.
    After seizing power, the Nazis reopened some of the formerly closed brothels
and started with their regulation (Evans, 1976, pp. 128). Already in 1933, prosti-
tution was forbidden in certain parts of a city (Hartmann, 2006, pp. 284) and leg-
islation, in general, became stricter (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 417). However, the Nazis’
intention was not to rule out all kinds of prostitution. On the one hand, brothels
were allowed, regulated, and after 1939, many women were forced to enter one of
these houses (Timm, 2002, p. 247). At the same time, a law dictated each city to
open a state run brothel (Roos, 2002, p. 89). During the next years, brothels for
the Wehrmacht (Timm, 2002, p. 247), for forced laborers (cited from Roos (2002,
p. 88)), and in the concentration camps (in 1942) opened (Timm, 2002, p. 247).
On the other hand, street prostitution was prosecuted harshly (Roos, 2002, p. 69)
4.3 Changes in the Law                                                            129

and after 1940, any kind of street-walking was forbidden (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 418).
The Nazis did not intend to ban prostitution completely, instead they used sexu-
ality for their ideology and tried to hide the commercial sex sector from public
view (Timm, 2002, p. 246). As a matter of fact, street-walkers were arrested and,
in the worst cases, sent to a concentration camp (cited from Höß (1963, p. 117)).
The average number of arrests in Hamburg already reﬂects that many were put into
jail or even worse. Behind closed doors the provision of prostitutes for men was
always ensured and women were, in most cases, forced to work in these houses
(Paul, 1994, p. 123). However, one has to keep in mind that after the ﬁrst years
of clearing the streets of prostitution, fewer women were offering services – es-
pecially on the streets – and arrest rates were rather low. It seems reasonable to
move the variable gIII to the very left of the ordering – there is no doubt that the
legislation became much stricter during these dark years of German history.
    After World War II, the country was separated into two parts. Hamburg, how-
ever, is not comparable to any city within the GDR nor is – to our best knowledge
– data on arrests available for the eastern part of Germany. Accordingly, the fourth
epoch has to be discussed in a rather isolated manner. Within the Russian zone of
occupation, prostitution was, in fact, legal until 1968 (Falck, 1998, p. 81), although
prostitutes had to fear preventive detention after 1945 (Hesse, 1991, p. 53). Legis-
lation about prostitution was rare during the years of the GDR, which was mostly
due to the denial of the existence of prostitution (see, e.g., Konieczka (1986, p.
108) and Korzilius (2005, pp. 196, 206, 405, 483)). This led, for many years, to
an actual impunity of prostitutes. In 1968, prostitution was banned and practiced
even more hidden from society (Falck, 1998, p. 14). In 1979, the state of affairs
was broadened and sentenced not only habitual offenders, but also women caught
for the ﬁrst time (Korzilius, 2005, p. 451). The supervision of prostitution by the
Stasi led to an incomparable situation: Women’s actions were, in fact, criminal,
but did not lead to prosecution. In contrast, imprisonment was rare and women
often worked under the approval of the Stasi (Falck, 1998, pp. 160). Women had
good earning possibilities (cited from Falck (1998, p. 144)) and raids were sel-
dom during the GDR. Nevertheless, prostitution was unquestionably forbidden in
the GDR. During most of the previously discussed epochs, at least parts of the
commercial sex sector were tolerated. This cannot be stated for the eastern part of
Germany and justiﬁes a rather high rank for the fourth epoch. However, compared
to the Nazi regime, the laws still seem to be less suppressing and more tolerant.
    Also in West Germany, everyday life was characterized by shortage and hunger
after the Second World War. One of the greatest fears of the Allies was high infec-
tion rates (Freund, 2001, p. 206) and to avoid that, the GeschlKrG was once again
enacted in 1953. With this law, repeated offenses could lead to imprisonment of
up to six months (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, p. 59). 20 years of regimentation followed
the reenactment of the GeschlKrG (Gleß, 1999, p. 107) and women were super-
130                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

vised as well as obliged to have regular health tests (Gleß, 1999, p. 112). The huge
about-turn ﬁnally came in 1974: Prostitution was, in general, not prohibited and
could not be forbidden by the police (Gleß, 1999, pp. 106), but certain rules had to
be followed (Gleß, 1999, p. 101). It seems that West Germany followed a steady
liberalization of the jurisdiction concerning prostitution (Hartmann, 2006, p. 276).
Until 2002 – or rather today – the commercial sex sector was far from being equal-
ized to other service sectors. However, both society and jurisdiction seem to have
opened up with the innovations of 1974 – the goals of controls were less often to
penalize but rather to medically supervise prostitution (Hartmann, 2006, p. 277).
A slow but steady decline of strictness emerged, which started during the German
hippie movement. Data on arrests in Hamburg due to illegal prostitution reﬂects
this change: The laws on prostitution were less strict during the ﬁfth epoch than
any of the previously discussed epochs.
    The steady decline of strictness continued during the next two epochs. After the
fall of the Berlin Wall, West German legislation was more or less also valid for the
eastern part. However, laws on prostitution were mostly under the responsibility
of German states and very diverse.134 To name just a few examples: In Frank-
furt/Main, violating forbidden areas could lead to imprisonment (Leopold et al.,
1994, p. 148); in Stuttgart, prostitution was illegal but mostly tolerated (Leopold
et al., 1994, p. 174); and in Rostock, the authorities did not even address to dam
prostitution (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 210). The year 1992 ﬁnally led to an important
liberalization: Husbands and boyfriends could not be sentenced anymore (Leopold
et al., 1994, p. 40) if their partner voluntarily worked in the commercial sex sector.
After 1997, the goal was to protect women instead of restricting (Hartmann, 2006,
p. 269) or correcting their behavior – even street prostitution was often tolerated
within forbidden areas (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 270). Until the ProstG came into
effect, the loosening up of the jurisdiction was not intended ofﬁcially, but cannot
be denied in practice. Bills concerning the equalization of prostitution were drafted
in by different parties (Müller, 2008, p. 18) – the ﬁrst in 1990 (Die Grünen, 1990,
p. 1) – and with that a long legislation process started. What has been stated for
the previous period is also true here: Laws concerning prostitution moved towards
liberalization, which is also reﬂected by the data on arrests in Hamburg.
    After January 1st , 2002, prostitution was no longer a crime nor a moral offense
(Renzikowski, 2009, p. 136). Instead, prostitution was equalized to any other pro-
fession in Germany (Kavemann, 2010, p. 214) and accepted to be the autonomous
work decision of a respective woman (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 6).
It is still possible to implement forbidden areas in Germany (Bundesministerium
für Familie, 2007, p. 74) and many cities and states make use of this possibility.

134 Due to the federal structure of Germany, the 16 states are able to organize certain parts of

legislation differently.
4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation                                     131

Often – as in Hamburg – offering services on the streets during certain times of
the day is forbidden (Senat Hamburg, 1980). But even with these restrictions, Ger-
many has, especially during the last years, adopted one of the most liberal laws
concerning prostitution in Europe (see, e.g., Müller (2008, p. 17)). The data on
arrests in Hamburg suggests that the variable for the years after the ProstG was en-
acted should be ranked higher than the one for the previous epoch. However, one
needs to keep in mind that since 2007, §184f StGB is also included in the statistics.
If we include only the years until 2006, we ﬁnd a much lower average of only 218
arrests per year for the seventh epoch. With that, the values for the last two eras are
almost equal. The liberal laws in Germany nowadays seem to allow an ordering of
gV II on the very right of the equation.
    During the last 167 years, the legislation on prostitution varied strongly, which
sheds light on how often this topic was on peoples’ minds. Looking at the arrests in
Hamburg due to illegal prostitution gave us a good starting point. After reviewing
and discussing the history of prostitution with a focus on the changes in laws,
speciﬁcs of German history are more obvious. Due to that, a reordering of the
variables seems to be appropriate. We suggest the following ordinal ordering:

                       gIII > gIV > gI > gII > gV > gV I > gV II .               (4.5)

In the following we discuss two variables that are very likely to be inﬂuenced by
the strictness in law. Section 4.4 focuses on one of the most difﬁcult variables to
measure: the possible loss of reputation.



4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation

In the following, we analyze how a possible loss of reputation r if caught selling
commercial sex has changed over time. In Chapter 2, we have deﬁned seven epochs
in the history of prostitution depending on the occurrence of important changes
in legislation or – in regards to the reuniﬁcation – the German society. We refer
to these seven periods when applying the model. To be able to give an ordinal
order between them, we need to ﬁnd a starting point for comparison. This shall be
discussed in the following.
132                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

4.4.1 Means of Measurement

Measuring the loss of reputation is a difﬁcult task. First, we need to recall what
the term loss of reputation means in our context. If women caught working as a
prostitute did not lose any reputation, there would not be a difference between
the market for commercial sex and any other labor market. Obviously, differences
must be present as women are compensated for working in the commercial sex
market – through receiving higher wages – and most active prostitutes feel stigma-
tized and try to hide their profession (SOFFI K., 2005b, p. 60).
    In reality, the loss of reputation most likely differs for each individual prostitute
(see, e.g., Della Giusta et al. (2006, p. 504)), depending on her social status, age,
education, earning capacities, and the behavior of her friends and family. Unfortu-
nately, it is – at least for us – impossible to measure all these variables and analyze
their inﬂuence on the behavior of prostitutes. Further research might contribute
to this. What is certain is, however, that the possible loss of reputation changed
over the last 167 years for the representative prostitute. This is mostly true as the
attitude towards sexuality – and with that prostitution – has developed since 1846.
    Looking at the history of prostitution, we notice that women’s rights are closely
connected to the attitude of society towards sexuality in general.135 We argue that
as womens’ rights increase, sexuality became more accepted.136 An obvious point
in favor of this argument is that the sexual self determination of women always
played a central role for feminists as well as the right to decide upon matters of
their own body.137 The acceptance of sexuality as a part of everyday life is crit-
ical for accepting prostitution as a profession and lowering the stigmatization of
women working in the commercial sex sector. Ergo, more women’s rights are taken
as an indicator for a lower loss of reputation if found out to be a sex worker.
    However, even measuring women’s standing in society is critical and anything
but straightforward. Nave-Herz (1997) and Haeberle (1985, pp. 328) provide good
overviews of the struggles of women to achieve an equalized standing in society.
In the ﬁrst years of the women’s rights movements, one of the major claims was
that the equalization of men and women was only possible if comparable education

135 See, e.g., Haeberle (1985, p. 311), who argues that an equalization is only possible if tra-

ditional role allocations in sexuality are overcome. Early feminists agreed (Haeberle, 1985, p.
324). Blaschko and Fischer (1913, p. 509) describe that the emancipation of women was seen
as one reason for more infections after the First World War and Eder (2009, pp. 40) states that
many authors discussing the late 19th century – especially Shorter (1971) – saw the high rate of
illegitimate children as an indication for more self-realization and an open sex life.
136 On the different standing of men and women in society, Haeberle (1985, pp. 309) gives a

great overview.
137 For a detailed discussion on women’s ﬁght against §218 StGB and the right of abortion,

EMMA (2001) shall be recommended.
4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation                                              133

opportunities were provided (Nave-Herz, 1997, p. 7). The foundation of the Ger-
man Women’s Association (Allgemeiner Deutscher Frauenverein, ADF) in 1865
can be seen as the beginning of the women’s movement in Germany (Haeberle,
1985, p. 329). Already in 1876, a petition was submitted, which demanded the
access to universities for women (Nave-Herz, 1997, p. 13). But, it took until 1900
for women to have the chance to enroll in tertiary education (see, e.g., Nave-Herz
(1997, p. 23) and Haeberle (1985, p. 329)). Crucial for the allowance of women
in lecture rooms was, among other factors, the fast industrialization of Germany
and, along with that, the lower earnings of the rural population and a higher share
of working women (Schulte, 1994, p. 68). One feared that the very low female
earnings (Haeberle, 1985, p. 336) might induce more engagement in the market
for commercial sex as the earning capacities were much better (see, e.g., Bölke
(1975, p. 17) and Bäumer (1939, p. 325)). In the following years, the equalization
of women was promoted, the number of members in the ADF increased (see, e.g.,
Evans (1979, p. 102) and Haeberle (1985, p. 329)), and since 1918, women have
the right to vote and be elected in Germany (see, e.g., Haeberle (1985, p. 333) and
Nave-Herz (1997, p. 26)). However, women’s rights were suppressed during the
Nazi regime and it was dictated that a woman’s only duty was to serve her hus-
band (Haeberle, 1985, pp. 333). Many associations quit their efforts during these
years (Nave-Herz, 1997, p. 31), but resumed work right after the end of the Sec-
ond World War (see, e.g., Nave-Herz (1997, p. 35) and Haeberle (1985, p. 334)).
In the eastern part of Germany, one could quote Marx,138 who claimed that "social
progress may be measured precisely by the social position of the fair sex (plain
ones included)" (Marx and Engels, 1974, pp. 582),139 but even there the equaliza-
tion of genders was far from complete (see, e.g., Nave-Herz (1997, pp. 65) and
Haeberle (1985, p. 334)). In West Germany, the equal standing of women and men
can be found in the German GG after 1949 (Nave-Herz, 1997, p. 37). From the
1968s movement on (Nave-Herz, 1997, pp. 39), women’s engagement in the po-
litical process grew steadily (Haeberle, 1985, pp. 334). But even in 1995, one of
the major demands remained: Equal opportunity and equal education for men and
women (Die Vierte Weltfrauenkonferenz, 1995).
    As a proxy for the standing of women in society, we make use of the central
demand of the feminist movement and look at the education of women over time.
The following graphs are based on data from the Statistisches Reichsamt (1936,
1939, 1940, 1941), the Staatliche Zentralverwaltung für Statistik (1956, 1962,
1969, 1974, 1976, 1989), gesis (2012), and the Statistisches Bundesamt (2013a).

138 A short reference to Marx’s relationship with his wife Jenny and her sacriﬁce during their

marriage shall be allowed (EMMA, 2001).
139 "Der gesellschaftliche Fortschritt läßt sich exakt messen an der gesellschaftlichen Stellung

des schönen Geschlchts (die häßlichen eingeschlossen)." (Marx and Engels, 1974, pp. 582)

The left panel of Figure 4.9 shows the development of the absolute number of stu-
dents in Germany. The gray line marks the students in the GDR, whereas the black
line represents the rest of Germany. The same line colors are chosen in the right
panel of Figure 4.9, which displays the absolute amount of female students only.
For both variables, we see an almost steady increase over time.140
    Of course, relative measures shown in Figure 4.9c are very interesting to apply.
In this graph, we see the development of the percentage of female students relative
to all students over time. After women were allowed to study in 1908, only 0.6% of
all students were female. In the following years, this share steadily increased until
1918. After the revolution, a small negative kink occured, but we then observe
a renewed steady increase until 1933. More men than women fell victim to the
Second World War and, accordingly, during the 1940s almost half of all students
were female. The share dropped dramatically at the end of World War II and was
at only 17% when the FRG was founded. But from 1947 onwards, the share of
female students increased year by year, reaching a level of 47.3% in 2011. In the
GDR, approximately 20% of all students were female after the Second World War.
The peak was reached in 1986 when more than half – 50.3% – of all students were
women. In the year of reuniﬁcation, the share was at 48.4% – more than 10% above
the level in the FRG. The starting point was comparable for both German sectors
and the share was for many years similar in East and West. During the 1970s and
1980s, however, it increased much faster in the GDR than in the FRG.
    For the sake of completeness, data for Hamburg has been analyzed exclusively.
Since all indicators – total number of students, female students, and percentage
of female students – show similar developments as the numbers for Germany as a
whole, we do not discuss the ﬁndings any further. However, the interested reader
ﬁnds the data in Appendix A.6 and a graphical illustration in Figures 4.10a, 4.10b,
and 4.10c.

It seems appropriate to recall the argumentation at this point: We argue that the loss
of reputation is higher the less sexuality is accepted within society. It is difﬁcult to
measure to what extent sexuality is accepted in society. Nevertheless, it is assumed
that a more open-minded society is also more accepting of sexuality. We chose
female education to measure the extent of equalization between men and women
– following the central demand of the vast majority of women’s rights activists.

Using the share of female students as an indicator for the loss of reputation over
time, we can now state an ordinal ordering for the seven epochs, as given below:

                         rI > rII > rIII > rV > rIV > rV I > rV II .                   (4.6)
From equation 4.6, we can draw one important and intuitive conclusion: The tol-
erance of society has increased steadily and the loss of reputation, accordingly,
decreased since 1846. That this development is expressed in equation 4.6 is due
to the fact that the number of female students in Germany has followed a certain
time-trend with a steady development towards equal education opportunities for
the genders. To conclude that the loss of reputation has also followed this time-
trend seems appropriate. In order to see if we ﬁnd proof for this conclusion, we
again look at the past, speciﬁcally the history of prostitution, in the following.

4.4.2 Looking Back

The ﬁrst period we observe is characterized by very diverse attitudes towards sex-
uality and prostitution. On the one hand, prostitution was widespread at the turn
of the century and a part of everyday life (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 48). Women selling
sex for money were visible everywhere, especially after the closing down of the
brothels (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 48). Due to this reason, one can assume that society
4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation                                              137

was used to living with prostitution. Additionally, if a woman chose this profes-
sion in order to support her family it was less stigmatized than one might expect as
fast industrialization led to worse living conditions for the poor (cited from Scott
and Tilly (1975, pp. 57)). On the other hand, sexuality in general was taboo within
society (Schulte, 1994, p. 137) and a woman working in prostitution was imme-
diately discredited. Of course, if a woman was detected to work in prostitution, it
was nearly impossible to regain the status of a decent woman and get married.
    In the years between 1900 and 1933, the biggest fear of the population was
venereal disease. It should not be a surprise that the loss of reputation was huge
for women working in prostitution as, at this time, one assumed an infection rate
of up to 50% for this profession (Blaschko and Fischer, 1913, p. 527). After 1905,
prostitutes were subject to medical treatments without their approval (Sauerteig,
1999, p. 347) under a procedure not comparable to the treatment we know today.
Often, being treated for having a veneral disease led to irreversible damage or the
poisoning of the patient (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 34). Keeping this in mind, one un-
derstands that the connection to venereal diseases and high infection rates induced
a high possible loss of reputation. However, the fear of venereal diseases was al-
ready present in the ﬁrst epoch: Jeannel (1869) dedicates his entire book to the
ﬁght against infections and Sauerteig (1999, pp. 30) states that Jeannel (1869) was
not the only doctor concerned about venereal disease at the turn of the century,
but rather, in the majority. It seems impossible, however, to judge whether the in-
fection rates were higher or lower during the ﬁrst epoch compared to the second
(Sauerteig, 1999, p. 72). During and after World War I, small attempts to make sex-
uality socially acceptable (Sauerteig, 1999, pp. 276) were made, which justiﬁes the
assumption of a slightly smaller r for the second period in comparison to the ﬁrst
period (as we have discussed). This is in line with the ﬁndings from Section 4.4.1.
    The major goal of the Nazis concerning prostitution was to hidden it from pub-
lic view (Timm, 2002, p. 237), which already sheds light on a possible loss of
reputation for the women. After seizing the power, the Nazis made propaganda
against prostitution and related professions (Roos, 2002, p. 69). On the one hand,
the women were expelled from society (Timm, 2002, p. 246) but on the other hand,
were required to serve the needs of men (Paul, 1994, p. 12). Accordingly, prostitu-
tion in brothels was tolerated by the government and after some years even society
stopped protesting against the regulation of prostitution (Timm, 2002, p. 242). In
Nazi Germany, the role of sexuality changed:
   "The totalitarian impulse to make even the most private of human activities serve national
   goals meant that Nazi leaders sought not only to deﬁne acceptable sexual behavior but
   to redeﬁne sexual acts as acts with public – not simply private – signiﬁcance. Sex and
   reproduction were crucial elements of population policy, indispensable in the formation
   of a strong state." (Timm, 2002, p. 223)
138                                                         4 An Application of the Meta-Model

Correspondingly, prostitution was more than a necessary evil that could be ig-
nored and suppressed: "During National Socialism, prostitutes were disenfran-
chised, criminalized, persecuted, and punished with rigid laws and methods" (Paul,
1994, p. 131, own translation).141 Women not only lost their reputation, but also
risked being sent to a concentration camp (Höß, 1963, p. 117) – a risk that cannot
be overrated. But even after years of war, women’s suffering did not come to an
end: One of the most surprising facts is that the pure existence of forced prostitu-
tion in concentration camps was denied for many years (see, e.g., Lagergemein-
schaft Ravensbrück (2001) and Paul (1994, pp. 85)) as it was shameful to have
visited one of these brothels (Kohl, 2010). It is doubtful that this denial helped
the surviving women cope (Paul, 1994, p. 91). If we took the education of women
as an indicator, we would have to assume that the loss of reputation was lower
during the Nazi regime than in both previously described epochs. The cruelty and
degradation of humans after the seizure of power, however, does not allow such a
conclusion. In addition, the data is misleading here as more men than women died
during the war, lowering the share of male students during and after the war. As a
matter of fact, we assume that the loss of reputation was never bigger than in Nazi
Germany.
   Postwar Germany was characterized by shortage and hunger. Many women of-
fered their services in order to feed their children and the number of prostitutes
rose rapidly. Respectively, prostitution was more or less accepted as a profession
and a way to survive (Falck, 1998, pp. 21). In 1946, the ﬁrst closed hospital wards
for infected prostitutes opened in the Russian zone of occupation (Günther, 1991b,
p. 166) and, after 1946, women caught selling sexual services more than once were
sent to one of the workhouses (Falck, 1998, p. 25). The partial acceptance of so-
ciety vanished after the ﬁrst war consequences were overcome and citizens often
informed the police if a woman was working as a prostitute (cited from Korzilius
(2005, p. 315)). Especially from a moral point of view, prostitution had a negative
image within the East German society (Korzilius, 2005, pp. 206), which becomes
obvious when looking at the deﬁnition of prostitution in Aresin et al. (1990, p. 160,
own translation):
   "Prostitution (lat prostituere = to expose somebody): Since the 15th /16th century a term for
   sexual actions in return for money or for gaining advantages. [...] Even if prostitution has
   changed over the years, its character remained: It is an expression of a moral double stan-
   dard and women are seen as a good. Prostitution takes place in hotels, massage parlours,
   saunas, in cars, on the street, where prostitutes always risk infection and to be criminalized
   by their clients. [...] The "work" of prostitutes is degrading, hard, and dangerous. This is
   why they are often addicted to drugs. The economic exploitation, the undigniﬁed services
   men often demand from prostitutes, the psychological consequences, the internalization

141 "Während des Nationalsozialismus wurden Prostituierte mit rigiden Gesetzen entrechtet,

kriminalisiert, verfolgt und bestraft." (Paul, 1994, p. 131)
4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation                                               139

   of self-contempt, self-hate are the major reasons why women’s movements ﬁght against
   prostitution. In the GDR, prostitution is forbidden."142

During the 1960s, the view of sexuality in the GDR changed (Hohmann, 1991,
p. 15) – prostitution was less visible for members of the society (Falck, 1998, p.
90) and still unwanted by the government (Falck, 1998, pp. 81). It seems that the
picture of prostitution within society was very diverse. Falck (1998, p. 160) states
that both attitudes were present: acceptance and viliﬁcation. With an increasing
acceptance of sexuality, the reprobation of prostitution declined over the years. The
loss of reputation decreased more in the GDR compared to all previous epochs,
which, again, is in line with the ﬁndings from Section 4.4.1, where we assumed a
comparatively lower loss of reputation due to the higher share of female students.
   The situation in the West after the Second World War was equivalent to the
one within the Russian zone of occupation. The ofﬁcials of the Allies intention-
ally created an environment that feared infections and therefore had strong moral
judgments against prostitution (Freund, 2001, p. 206). The increasing number of
prostitutes alarmed the authorities (Freund, 2001, p. 207), but as the phenomenon
was so widespread (Tröger, 1986, p. 98), society must have known and, at least to
some extent, accepted this behavior (Falck, 1998, pp. 21). Tröger (1986, pp. 114)
explains how West German women went back to traditional role allocations after
the ﬁrst war consequences were overcome – accordingly, prudery again increased
in the German society (see, e.g., Heinz-Trossen (1993, p. 51) and Tröger (1986,
p. 115)). Nevertheless, prostitution never vanished completely from the West Ger-
man society – it was seen as a necessity that should not be banned (Gleß, 1999,
p. 132), but rather kept out of sight (Renzikowski, 2009, p. 134). Over the years,
the acceptance of sexuality as something natural increased, mainly driven by the
movement in 1968 and an increase in women’s rights (see, e.g., Nave-Herz (1997,
p. 40) and Haeberle (1985, pp. 334)). Looking at the data, we found that the share

142 "Prostitution (lat prostituere = jemand bloßstellen, entehren): Seit dem 15./16 Jh. Bezeich-

nung für die Vornahme sexueller Handlungen gegen Entgelt oder zur Erlangung von Vorteilen.
Die ethnolog. Wurzel verweist auf den Zusammenhang mit der Verfolgung von Prostituierten,
den Hexenpogromen, dem Pranger für Prostituierte. Seit dem 18. Jh. wird das Wort Prostituieren
im Sinne "sich prostituieren" gebraucht. Damit ist in erster Linie das Anbieten und Verkaufen
sexueller Handlungen gemeint. Auch wenn die P. sich im Laufe der Jahre verändert hat, ist ihr
Charakter gleich geblieben: Sie ist Ausdruck von Doppelmoral u. Betrachtung der Frau als Ware.
P. wird in Stundenhotels, Massagesalons, Saunen, im Auto, auf der Straße betrieben, wobei die
Prostituierten ständig Gefahr laufen, durch ihre Kunden krank gemacht u. kriminalisiert zu wer-
den. Zuhälter beuten die Prostituierten unter dem Vorwand ihres Schutzes zusätzl. aus. Die ’Ar-
beit’ von Prostituierten ist entwürdigend, hart u. gefährl. Oft zählen sie deshalb zu den Drogen-
abhängigen. Die wirtschaftl. Ausbeutung, die würdelosen Dienste, die Männer von Prostituierten
verlangen, die psychischen Folgen, die Verinnerlichung von Selbstverachtung, Selbsthaß sind die
wesentlichsten Gründe, weshalb die Frauenbewegung gegen die P. kämpfen. In der DDR ist die
P. verboten." (Aresin et al., 1990, p. 160)
140                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

of female students increased slower in West Germany than in the East. Drawing
from this relationship the conclusion that a loss of reputation for women work-
ing in prostitution was smaller in the East than in the West might be, at least for
our understanding of the topic, misleading. Firstly, the West German legislation
decriminalized prostitution in 1974, which is a sharp contrast to the attitude of
the East German government. In the eastern part, the possible loss of reputation
was probably higher than often described (see, e.g., Falck (1998, pp. 153)) – if
the existence of prostitution was not denied (see, e.g., Korzilius (2005, pp. 196,
206, 405, 483)), it was certainly discredited by decision makers in the East (see,
e.g., Korzilius (2005, pp. 18, 201, 205, 273)). It does not seem comprehensible to
assume that this did not have a strong inﬂuence on the attitude of society in East
Germany towards prostitution (Falck, 1998, p. 170). To ignore the possible impact
both jurisdictions had on the respective society seems to underestimate the guiding
effect laws often have on society and vice versa (see, e.g., Stanford Encyclopedia
of Philosophy (2001)). In addition, the acceptance of sexuality as a part of every-
day life appears to have proceeded faster in the West than in the East (Falck, 1998,
p. 97). Finally, one should notice that the equalization of men and women was not
as complete as the numbers of female students within the GDR suggest: Between
1949 and 1989, only four women were appointed as minister (see, e.g., Nave-Herz
(1997, pp. 65) and Haeberle (1985, p. 334)) and even the labor market often fol-
lowed traditional gender roles (Nave-Herz, 1997, p. 65). Taking this into account,
we assume a loss of reputation for West Germany that is lower compared to all
preceding epochs (including the GDR).
    Slowly but steadily, the loss of reputation declined in the reunited Germany. Al-
though West German prostitutes formed the ﬁrst movements comparable to unions
already in the 1970s (von Galen, 2004, p. 6), stigmatization was still present. Even
during the 1990s, women felt expelled from society (Stallberg, 1988, p. 63) and
had to accept living in a half-legal environment (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 1). One
denied the possibility of voluntary prostitution (Hohmann, 1991, p. 37) and con-
sequently, the legislator did not want to allow an action he considered to be forced
prostitution. Nevertheless, the question of equalizing prostitution came back into
the focus of the legislator. Three out of the ﬁve major political parties handed in
bills demanding the termination of the legal and moral suppression of sex workers
(Müller, 2008, p. 18). Radical changes of such signiﬁcance are seldom possible
over night. In Germany, it took 12 years from the ﬁrst drafted bill to reach the legal
equalization of prostitution (Müller, 2008, p. 18), but many intermediate changes
accompanied this path: In the late 1990s, the goal of legislation changed to protect
sex workers as well as to decriminalize actions supporting prostitution (Hartmann,
2006, pp. 268). In the millennium year, courts started to question whether moral
principles were violated by commercial sex (Kavemann, 2010, p. 213). The ProstG
came into effect in 2002 with the goal to equalize prostitution to all other profes-
4.4 Changes in the Possible Loss of Reputation                                                141

sions and to allow the women to legally engage in the profession (von Galen, 2004,
pp. 102). The years before the ProstG received a majority in the Bundestag were
characterized by long and intense discussions on this topic (von Galen, 2004, p.
10). However, neither was sexuality in general kept quiet nor was the right to chose
prostitution as a profession denied. As a comparison of the share of female students
over the years suggests, we agree to assign a lower possible loss of reputation to
this sixth epoch compared to all previously discussed periods.
    With the ProstG, prostitution does not violate moral principles of society any-
more – at least not legally (von Galen, 2004, p. 126). One of the major goals
was to accept prostitution as an autonomous decision of the respective woman by
equalizing it to all other professions (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 8).
However, women still feel stigmatized if discovered to work in the commercial sex
sector (SOFFI K., 2005b, pp. 200) and it seems that society has not yet decided
how to treat prostitution as a part of the community (Bundesministerium für Fami-
lie, 2007, p. 8). As stigmatization is still present, the fear of being caught selling
commercial sex is still non negligible (SOFFI K., 2005b, pp. 200). Nevertheless,
one quarter of all interviewed prostitutes claims that they would like to be more
honest about their profession and are planning to do so in the future (SOFFI K.,
2005b, p. 196). The introduction of the new law gave many women hope of a bet-
ter and safer working environment (SOFFI K., 2005b, p. 196), although they did
not observe considerable changes in society or administrative ofﬁces, at least not
two years after the law came into effect (SOFFI K., 2005b, p. 195). The loss of
reputation is decreasing especially because the majority of sex workers seem to
appreciate their new legal rights (SOFFI K., 2005b, p. 198). Overall, prostitutes
still fear a loss of reputation if seen selling commercial sex, even though no le-
gal consequences occur.143 Compared to previous epochs, the loss of reputation
should be considered comparatively small and steadily decreasing. Again, this is
in line with our ﬁndings from Section 4.4.1.
    After having critically reviewed the history of prostitution in terms of the ac-
ceptance of sexuality in general and sex work in particular, it seems that the proxy
we chose for measuring the loss of reputation is acceptable. Looking solely at the
share of female students, the ordinal ordering as given in equation 4.6 is suggested.
Our understanding of the topic and its history helps us draw the conclusion that
only two parameters should be ranked differently than in equation 4.6 – namely
rIII and rIV . Both changes are induced by the rather unique history of Germany
and leave us with the following ordering:

                         rIII > rI > rII > rIV > rV > rV I > rV II .                        (4.7)

143 In fact, this is not entirely true as forbidden areas are still possible and often declared in

Germany.
142                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

With the exception of the epoch for the Nazi regime, equation 4.7 also expresses
the already mentioned time-trend the loss of reputation followed: During the last
167 years the tolerance of society seems to have increased steadily and the loss of
reputation decreased, although it did not vanish completely.



4.5 Changes in the Probability of Getting Caught

In Chapter 3, we deﬁned prostitution as a risky profession. Throughout this work,
we assume that women have a secure labor alternative – working in a sector v
– and that women lose something after being caught selling commercial sex. In
the previous section, we discussed what we mean by this something: reputation.
The probability of getting caught should play a major role when women decide
which labor market they wish to participate in, as this variable gives them the
information regarding how likely it is that they will lose their reputation. We start
by discussing how to measure the variable in focus and, in a second step, review
the history of prostitution to see if the measure applied can be used to model the
respective market.



4.5.1 Means of Measurement

When thinking about how to measure a probability of getting caught, different
possibilities come to mind. To name just a few – the share of indoor prostitution
or the number of raids could be viable measures. However, none of these variables
are available over the period of time we are looking at. In general, we have to admit
that ﬁnding one variable that we are able to measure over the entire 167 years is
close to impossible. Instead, we have to work with only some observations and
less comparability than optimal.
   The probability of getting caught during certain years is closely related to the
presence of police in the city. However, one needs to question whether it is only
relevant if women are caught by the authorities or also by friends and acquain-
tances. We have argued before that individuals care, in fact, more about how their
behavior is judged by their peer group than of an abstract society (see, e.g., Galí
(1994) and Barbour et al. (2013)). However, there is no comprehensible data set
on social assessments within families.
   As an indicator for the probability of getting caught, we take the number of po-
lice ofﬁcers in Hamburg – in absolute terms and per 1,000 inhabitants. The federal
structure of Germany dictates us to limit our analysis to one state only as the police
4.5 Changes in the Probability of Getting Caught                                                        143

is under the competency of each state individually. Hamburg was again chosen to
keep the data as comparable as possible, as well as the existence of restricted ar-
eas. Unfortunately, not even data on the police members is available for the whole
period of time we are analyzing. In contrast to many other states, Hamburg does
not provide statistics on the employees of the police for the past few years. After
many unsuccessful requests, the data was compiled in Kiel for the years after the
Second World War. All other data was taken from the historical annual abstracts
of statistics, but data for the ﬁrst epoch is not available at all.144 All other data is
given in Appendix A.7.

A graphical illustration of the police force in Hamburg – in absolute terms – is
given in Figure 4.12a, whereas the right panel of Figure 4.12 shows the develop-
ment per 1,000 inhabitants. The years where data was missing have been excluded
to avoid jumps in the graph. In addition, the number for the year 1938 has not been
taken into account. From the Statistisches Landesamt (1938), we ﬁnd a statement
that 429 people were working for the Hamburg police. However, we also ﬁnd a
note explaining that employees were part of the Reich and not of the state Ham-
burg during that year – the comparatively low number does not seem to express
the real situation in Hamburg in 1938. All other data includes the numbers for civil
servants, employees, and workers.145 The lowest numbers in absolute terms are
reported for the years 1914 and 1925 with less than 5,000 members of the police
force. Also low – in fact, the lowest on average – are the absolute numbers for the
period of the Third Reich. This should not be surprising as these were years of war
144 Section 5.2, discusses this problem again.
145 In German, one distinguishes between Beamte, Angestellte, and Arbeiter.

and the policemen joined the armed forces. In addition, other security units were
present during this time. After the Second World War, the police seems to have
steadily increased their staff until the year 1987. During the following years, the
number ﬂuctuated between almost 11,000 and a little over 9,000 police members.
Per 1,000 inhabitants, the lowest numbers occur for the years 1925 and 1937 and
the highest for the year 1920. Interestingly, when looking at the relative number of
policemen, the Third Reich is no longer responsible for the lowest average but the
years between the Second World War and the fall of the Berlin Wall are.

Of course, the number of policemen in Hamburg can only give us a ﬁrst impression
of which priority crimes had for the state during different time periods. In addition,
prostitution is far from being the only crime authorities try to regulate and, thus,
an increasing number does not necessarily mean that the prostitutes were affected
or that it was induced by an expansion of the market for commercial sex. Certainly
true is, however, that with more policemen, women bear a higher risk of being
detected offering commercial sex – not only when working on the street, but also
during raids and controls of indoor areas.
    To judge whether one of the epochs was characterized by a higher probability
of getting caught, we have to look at the average number of staff members per
1,000 inhabitants for each epoch. These numbers are given in Table 4.6. During
the second epoch, the police force had, in comparison, most employees with 7.67
policemen per 1,000 inhabitants. With only 5.27 policemen per 1,000 inhabitants,
the years of separation are responsible for the lowest share. Second lowest is the
number for the years since the ProstG was enacted, which is only slightly lower
than during the third epoch. Figure 4.13 again illustrates these ﬁndings.
Before giving a ﬁrst ordinal ordering, we should try to make a statement about the
ﬁrst epoch. Excluding the years of Nazi power in Germany, we see an increase
in the absolute number of policemen, which is in line with an increasing total
