population for Hamburg in general.146 Urban (1927, pp. 56) and Röhrmann (1846,
p. 225) discuss that in the very ﬁrst years of the Hamburg vice squads, only very
few were responsible for the market for commercial sex. Of course, our previously
discussed data does not only include the vice squad, but also looks at the total
of employees of the Hamburg police. However, assuming a lower probability of
getting caught with a lower number of policemen in the ﬁrst epoch compared to
the second epoch seems reasonable. Accordingly, as a ﬁrst ordering, we suggest

                           ρ II > ρ I > ρ V I > ρ III > ρ V II > ρ V .          (4.8)

In the following, we look at the history of prostitution by focusing on the proba-
bility of getting caught to see whether this ordering is worth keeping or if it needs
revising. At the end of Section 4.5.2 we are able to judge the measure chosen here
as being appropriate or not.



4.5.2 Looking Back

During the end of the 19th century, the police struggled with – among other things
– a low stock of policemen who were able to supervise the commercial sex sector

146 The data on the population in Hamburg is given in Appendix A.1.
146                                                    4 An Application of the Meta-Model

(Röhrmann, 1846, p. 225). Despite low numbers, these few tried to contain pros-
titution and arrested many suspects (Röhrmann, 1846, p. 238). After 1867, police
raids happened more often in Berlin and the amount of arrests rose (cited from
Lacroix and Helbing (1995, p. 100)).147 Only nine years later, in 1876, women
were registered as prostitute against their will (Gleß, 1999, p. 59) and their apart-
ments could be searched in their absence (Stursberg, 1887, p. 35). In contrast to
Berlin, it seems that Hamburg was much more tolerant: Often, the only interven-
tion from the police was for tax collection (Jeannel, 1869, p. 181) and some streets
were, in fact, not supervised at all (cited from Lacroix and Helbing (1995, p. 86)).
This was in general a common procedure: The police tolerated prostitution in some
parts of the city to keep it away from other areas. The goal of keeping the com-
mercial sex sector away from the public view (Evans, 1976, p. 112) was much
easier reached if the transactions only took place in the small and narrow allays of
the historic section (Evans, 1976, p. 109). Until 1889, many cities allowed open
brothels in order to prevent a further rise of street prostitution (Evans, 1976, p.
110). But by the end of the 19th century, prostitution was very common especially
in Hamburg and Berlin (Schulte, 1994, pp. 22). The police force was enlarged, but
the control and supervision of such a big market was almost impossible, especially
as women were very mobile (Evans, 1976, pp. 112, 114). Summing up, one must
admit that the pure size of the market probably often prevented women from being
discovered to work in the commercial sex sector. Besides the risk of being detected
by the legal authorities, the knowledge about a possible engagement in the mar-
ket for commercial sex spread comparatively slow among friends and family with
only few information platforms available. Therefore, a rather small probability of
getting caught seems to be a reasonable assumption.
    Working outside as a prostitute was never very convenient. As a matter of fact,
many professional prostitutes decided to work in a brothel during the early years
of the new century (Gleß, 1999, p. 84). Just like the rest of the market, brothels
were regulated by the police and, consequently, the probability of getting caught
was relatively high (Gleß, 1999, p. 90). With the outbreak of the war, the police
was in charge of supervising the prostitutes (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 391). However,
the health of soldiers was seen as an important element to win the war. Ergo,
the strength of the police was extended (Evans, 1976, pp. 127) and, in addition,
the military started to supervise prostitutes (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 392). Despite a
larger number of men, it became more difﬁcult to monitor women as many broth-
els closed and street-walking made it easier to work undetected (Sauerteig, 1999,
p. 391). After the revolution of 1918/19, prostitutes were less supervised (Evans,
1976, p. 128) and with the GeschlKrG, convictions due to prostitution declined

147 The same trend can be found in the numbers of arrests in Hamburg after 1868 – please see

Appendix A.4.
4.5 Changes in the Probability of Getting Caught                                 147

(cited from Roos (2002, pp. 72)). After the GeschlKrG was enacted, the health
authorities became responsible for prostitution – an authority which was less able
to cope with the situation (Sauerteig, 1999, pp. 411). Nevertheless, the during the
war years increased, supervision seems to outweigh the decrease in control after
the GeschlKrG came into effect. Accordingly, compared to the previously ana-
lyzed epoch, women working in prostitution seem to be confronted with a higher
probability of getting caught during the ﬁrst years of the 20th century, which is in
line with our assumptions from Section 4.5.1.
    Immediately after the seizure of power, the Nazis started to make propaganda
against prostitution and connected it not only to venereal diseases (Roos, 2002, p.
79), but also to one of the greatest fears of the population – during the ﬁrst years
of the Nazi regime, the majority of the street-walkers were sent to concentration
camps. The probability of getting caught was, especially for prostitutes working on
the streets, huge (Timm, 2002, pp. 227). The number of arrests rose due to stricter
law enforcement (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 417) and 3,201 women were arrested be-
tween March and August 1933 in Hamburg alone (Freund-Widder, 2003, p. 115).
The reopening of brothels (Evans, 1976, p. 128) as well as the pressure to only
live in certain parts of the city led to the easier supervision of women (Roos, 2002,
p. 83). The Olympic Games of 1936 even induced an increased effort to clear the
streets of all remaining prostitutes (see, e.g., Timm (2002, p. 236) and Höß (1963,
p. 117)). During the following years, the suppression and supervision of asocials
increased (Paul, 1994, p. 29) and, after 1939, the police became even more present
(see, e.g., Gleß (1999, p. 96) and Roos (2002, p. 87)). Finally, street-walking was
more and more dangerous during the 1940s as the jurisdiction tightened (Timm,
2002, p. 249). In general, one has to notice that the control and inﬂuence of the
Nazis was endless and the probability of getting caught, accordingly, was huge.
Compared to the other epochs, the presence of the police and other authorities dur-
ing the Nazi regime, such as the SS and the Secret State Security (Geheime Staat-
spolizei, Gestapo), was never higher and thus, the probability of getting caught
should also be ranked the highest. The opposite conclusion would be drawn if we
were to only look at the number of policemen in Hamburg. However, the transfer
of men to the military as well as the presence of the SS and the Gestapo seem to
falsify the data. The Nazi regime marks very special – in a negative sense – years
in German history. Thus, a reordering seems to be appropriate.
    The GDR and Hamburg are not comparable when it comes to indicators and a
qualitative analysis of the situation in the GDR might be the only starting point.
It seems that the probability of getting caught was high during the Russian zone
of occupation and, in particular, later within the GDR. From 1943 onwards, con-
trols were more often as the military was looking for people infected with diseases
(Hesse, 1991, p. 52). The authorities focused mainly on women as carriers and,
therefore, the new rules basically only affected women (Korzilius, 2005, pp. 30).
148                                                4 An Application of the Meta-Model

In 1945, the Russian military declared the goal to ban prostitution from society in
general (see, e.g., Korzilius (2005, p. 88) and Hesse (1991, p. 53)) and, accord-
ingly, increased efforts to ﬁnd women working in the commercial sex sector. In
the following year, 358 people were sent to preventive detention and 2,438 were
forced into medical treatment in Thuringia alone (Hesse, 1991, p. 54). Raids took
place more often throughout the whole country (Günther, 1991b, p. 166): In 1946,
30,000 women were controlled in Brandenburg and 14,705 were arrested in Berlin
for being infected while working as prostitutes (cited from Korzilius (2005, p.
90)). Starting in the mid-1950s, the number of arrested prostitutes began to de-
cline (cited from Korzilius (2005, p. 204)) and reached a low in 1958 of only 35
arrests in Berlin (Falck, 1998, p. 41). In the 1970s, the Stasi was in charge of the
supervision of the commercial sex sector (Falck, 1998, p. 191) and with that the
number of sentenced suspects slightly increased (Korzilius, 2005, p. 444). Street
prostitution was only possible in Berlin and Leipzig and as brothels were very
rare, business mainly occurred in public places, if not even out in the open (Falck,
1998, p. 163). Many prostitutes worked under the approval of the Stasi and un-
known controls were seldom during the last years of the GDR (Falck, 1998, pp.
160, 191). However, the surveillance apparatus of the GDR was immense and the
large number of Unofﬁcial Collaborators (Inofﬁzieller Mitarbeiter, IM) leaves us
with the impression that exposure was very likely if working as a prostitute in the
GDR. Furthermore, the number of active prostitutes was rather small in the east-
ern part of Germany (Korzilius, 2005, p. 444), which makes it almost impossible
to disappear in a crowd. What is questionable is, however, whether the probability
of getting caught was larger during the GDR or the years of National Socialism.
Although the Nazis were very organized, the intensive years of war might have
kept their attention on areas other than prostitution. Correspondingly, it seems
reasonable to assume that detection was even more likely when working in the
commercial sex sector of the GDR.
    The probability of getting caught in West Germany ﬂuctuated strongly through-
out the ages. The East and West of postwar Germany faced the same problem:
Thousands of women offered their services (Tröger, 1986, p. 99) and hunger pros-
titution was very common (Freund, 2001, p. 206). The police actions started to
be more repressive as the authorities saw no other way to lower the visibility of
prostitution (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, p. 51) – in 1945, 6,562 women were temporar-
ily arrested in Hamburg (cited from Freund (2001, p. 210)). High infection rates
and the spread of the venereal diseases were still the biggest concern. After the
German authorities were put in charge of the supervision of the commercial sex
sector in 1953, their efforts were mainly focused on prostitution and its side ef-
fects, as they saw it as the origins of infections (Freund, 2001, p. 213). In fact,
there was no legal foundation for the supervision of prostitution in Germany after
1927, but that did not lower the efforts of police squads (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, pp.
4.5 Changes in the Probability of Getting Caught                                 149

62). Especially during the 1950s and 1960s, prostitution was actively regulated by
the police (Gleß, 1999, p. 107). By controlling women’s health certiﬁcates (Gleß,
1999, p. 112), the authorities intended to lower infection rates, which was still a
major goal. The supervision of prostitution in Germany was not too difﬁcult as
legal places to do business were limited (Gleß, 1999, pp. 139) – prostitutes had to
report moving immediately and tested for HIV regularly (Heinz-Trossen, 1993, p.
78). There is no doubt that the very ﬁrst years after the Second World War were
characterized by far reaching supervision and a focus on the market for commer-
cial sex as authorities "linked prostitution not only with morals but also with the
spread of VD" (Freund, 2001, p. 207). However, the jurisdiction relaxed after the
consequences of the war were overcome (Hartmann, 2006, p. 276). Accordingly,
raids occurred less often, fewer women were arrested, and the market was more
accepted within society. In particular, the effect the 1974 law had on society, and
vice versa, should not be underestimated. However, looking at the data, we see a
steadily increasing number of policemen per 1,000 inhabitants between 1946 and
1989 leading to the highest share of all post-World War II years during the 1980s.
In addition, compared to the previous epochs, communication platforms became
more important and knowledge started to spread faster. Assuming a higher prob-
ability of getting caught for the ﬁfth than during the ﬁrst and second epoch but
lower than during the third and fourth epoch, seems to be reasonable.
   The federal structure of Germany led to very different forms of supervising the
market for commercial sex after the reuniﬁcation and therefore, the probability of
getting caught varied intensely between the 16 states. The market was one of the
most diverse in Hamburg, including prostitution on the streets, in brothels, and
apartments (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 64). The Hanseatic police was quite tolerant
and the registered offenses declined during the 1990s (Leopold et al., 1994, p.
73), with a steadily decreasing level ever since. Berlin, another very tolerant city
(and state), did not have any restricted areas (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 8). However,
the number of women working on the streets still declined there during the 1990s
(Leopold et al., 1994, p. 88). In contrast, Frankfurt/Main deﬁned restricted areas
and the violation of these could lead to imprisonment. Legal prostitution was still
possible if performed in one of the tolerated zones (Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 148).
Stuttgart did not allow prostitution ofﬁcially, but did not punish it either (Leopold
et al., 1994, p. 174) – raids were still common in Swabia and women needed to
carry a health certiﬁcate at all times (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 180). Imprisonment
was also possible if violating a restricted area in Munich, although the major goal
was rather to punish related crimes than the women (Leopold et al., 1994, pp. 193).
The commercial sex sector was, compared to the city’s size, large in Rostock and
the market was more supervised than suppressed there (Leopold et al., 1994, p.
210). In general, one can state that street prostitution was often tolerated by the
police and imprisonment due to prostitution was rather seldom (Leopold et al.,
150                                                                                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

1994, pp. 270). In Section 4.5.1, we found that the probability of getting caught
was rather high during the sixth epoch, which seems to be appropriate as the vast
majority of the German states supervised the market during this period. In addition,
one major innovation has been ignored so far: the Internet.148 A very interesting
study was carried out by ARD/ZDF (2012) for the years since 1997, which re-
ported the absolute number of Internet users in Germany as well as the fraction of
the total number of households.149 The study is based on a representative sample
of around 2,000 individuals per year (ARD/ZDF, 2012) and displayed in Figures
4.14a and 4.14b. Figure 4.14a shows the development of the absolute number of
households with Internet in Germany since 1997, whereas Figure 4.14b shows the
changes in percentage of Internet users relative to all households in Germany.

We immediately see that the trend is almost steadily increasing. Data provided on-
line – such as advertisements and experience reports – are always available and
we have to keep in mind that it is much easier to communicate in the era of email.
Once a woman is found to be working in the commercial sex sector, word often
spreads much faster than before, making concealment more difﬁcult. Accordingly,
ρ V I should be ranked higher than the parameters for the previous epochs but lower
than the one for the Nazi regime and the GDR.
    Today, all forms of prostitution are legalized throughout Germany (von Galen,
2004, p. 128), although raids still occur with the goal to arrest pimps rather than

149 Similar data is available from Eurostat (2013) but as the numbers there are available only

since 2002, we chose to use data provided by ARD/ZDF (2012) as the trend is quite similar.
4.5 Changes in the Probability of Getting Caught                                              151

the women (SOFFI K., 2005a, p. 110). Nowadays, the market for commercial sex
is still supervised – mostly explained by the concerns regarding protection of the
youth (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 53) and it seems that it still vio-
lates the moral principles of many (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 13).
Even with the ProstG, restricted areas are allowed in Germany, leading to more
possibilities to increase the control of the market for commercial sex (Bundesmi-
nisterium für Familie, 2007, p. 74). However, women seem to have gained some
more conﬁdence as they voluntarily call the police if necessary (Holznagel, 2009,
p. 237). It seems reasonable to assume that the probability of getting caught has
decreased since the ProstG was enacted, as the legal obligation for the police to
supervise the market has been abolished – at least partly. We argued before that
the mobility of women (Evans, 1976, pp. 112, 114) often prevented detection, but
this seems to be impossible with the increasing presence of the Internet. With the
so-called Web 2.0,150 anonymity is today even less likely and the spread of knowl-
edge is much easier. With Facebook, Twitter, and other social networks, the world
has shrunk. Since 2002, the share of households with connection to the Internet
has increased from 44.1% to 75.9% (ARD/ZDF, 2012). In addition, going online
seems to be a part of everyday life, as in 2012 74.5% of the questioned agents
report to have used the Internet within the last four weeks (ARD/ZDF, 2012).
This is an increase of more than 23% in 10 years. With an increasing presence
of the Internet, many markets – among them the market for commercial sex –
have changed substantially. Instead of creating a substitute to commercial sex via
offering a broader audience to ﬁnd a partner for sexual intercourse without any
consequences, it seems that the Internet led to a new market segment for sellers
and buyers. Nevertheless, feedback from such profound changes on the traditional
market should be assumed and they might have caused some replacement shift
away from street prostitution (Cunningham and Kendall, 2011, p. 281). In addi-
tion, Cunningham and Kendall (2011, p. 275) argue that the increasing usage of
the Internet creates a new platform for advertisement and, at the same time, a
platform for sharing experiences and building up reputation for sex workers and
their customers. Besides, Cunningham and Kendall (2011, p. 275) assume that the
commercial sex sector has experienced an increase in supply and demand since the
revolution of the Internet.151 With all that in mind and having assumed a higher
probability of getting caught for the previous epoch, it seems reasonable that ρ V II
should even exceed ρ V I .


150 The idea of Web 2.0, for example, is discussed in O’Reilly (2007).
151 Please note that Cunningham and Kendall (2011, p. 276) refer in their study to a lower proba-

bility of being arrested by the authorities, whereas we are more interested in how the probability
of being caught by society has changed.
152                                                   4 An Application of the Meta-Model

    By now, we have suggested a measure for the variable ρ (the probability of
getting caught) and have, in a second step, critically reviewed the history of pros-
titution to see whether proof for the applied instrument can be found. Equation 4.4
suggests an ordinal ordering based only on the number of policemen in Hamburg.
Now, at the end of Section 4.5.2, we see a need to change some of the variables:
It seems that neither the increasing presence and inﬂuence of the Internet nor the
particularities of the Third Reich have been captured in the data on policemen
in Hamburg. In addition, what concerns us most about the probability of getting
caught is to express how easily family, friends, and acquaintances discover the se-
cret profession of a woman. Following a time-trend, it became much more conve-
nient to communicate during the last epochs and the probability of getting caught
does not only depend on the number of policemen anymore. With that in mind, it
is reasonable to reorder the succession slightly. Accordingly, it shall be suggested
that the following order is applied:

                   ρ IV > ρ III > ρ V II > ρ V I > ρ V > ρ II > ρ I .             (4.9)

In Section 4.6, we discuss whether we are able to show certain kinds of correlations
between the variables discussed before.



4.6 Finding and Discussing Correlations

In the previous section, we identiﬁed and discussed ﬁve relevant variables. First,
we discussed statistics on registered prostitutes in Hamburg as well as estimations
of the total number of sex workers. In order to make a statement on how the prices
have changed, we compared prices on the market for commercial sex to the price
for rye bread. We used the number of arrests in Hamburg to measure how the laws
concerning prostitution have changed. We looked at the share of female students
to evaluate how high the possible loss of reputation was for women offering sex-
ual services. The probability of getting caught was measured by the number of
policemen in Hamburg.
    Not discussed so far has been how these variables interact with each other and
how they inﬂuence one another. Scatter diagrams help us to detect possible corre-
lations and outliers at the same time. The term correlation is used here although it
is not understood in the statistical meaning, but rather in the literal sense.
4.6 Finding and Discussing Correlations                                           153

4.6.1 Law Correlations

One of the main motivations of the work at hand, was to give insight into the
changing laws concerning prostitution in Germany within the last 167 years. Thus,
beginning the analysis of possible correlations with the variable g seems to be
straightforward. We certainly do not argue that all of the variables we analyze
depend or inﬂuence one another – we assume dependencies only between some. It
is, however, true that society has an inﬂuence on how laws change and the legislator
seems to give society a framework of what is accepted and what is not throughout
the laws enacted (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, 2001). Therefore, looking
at dependencies between the law and the probability of getting caught as well as
the loss of reputation increases our understanding of the topic.

All variables we are interested in have been ordered in ordinal equations during the
previous sections. In this section, we show correlations between pairs of variables
in a scatter diagram. First, we need to assign coordinates for each of the seven
epochs. In order to do so, we use equations 4.5, 4.7, as well as 4.9 and give the
value one to the variable on the very right of the respective equation and a seven
for the one on the very left. Note that all other values would work here, as we are
not interested in differences in the value but only in ordering. With that, we are
able to set up Table 4.7 and Table 4.8.
   The ﬁrst column of Table 4.7 and Table 4.8, respectively, gives the ordering of
variable g as found in equation 4.5. The second column is then different for both
tables – in Table 4.7, we see the order of the loss of reputation and in Table 4.8 the
one for the probability of getting caught. The rank, in the following column, as-
signs values to the different epoch variables. The epochs are then listed in column
154                                                                                               4 An Application of the Meta-Model

four and the last column states the coordinates needed to create a scatter diagram.
Both scatter diagrams are shown in Figure 4.15a and Figure 4.15b.
    The left panel of Figure 4.15 shows how a possible loss of reputation is related
to the strictness of laws within one epoch. What we see is not surprising: With
stricter laws, the loss of reputation increases and vice versa. This illustrates again
how much legislation and attitude of society depend on one another. The only out-
lier is the mark for the fourth epoch – the years of the GDR. Although, the laws
were comparatively strict, the loss of reputation was evaluated to be relatively low.
Women’s rights – an important topic during the years of separation – are the reason
for this lower rank. One has to note that the political system was a very different
one within the GDR. Showing interdependencies – and with that showing corre-
lations – is certainly possible for years which are comparable among each other.
However, the Nazi regime, as well as the years of the GDR, are characterized by
quite different political frameworks and the extent to which they are comparable to
the other epochs is rather limited. Ergo, it should not be surprising that these two
epochs will mostly form an exception for the following scatter diagrams.

                       Fig. 4.15: Scatter Diagrams for g and r as well as g and ρ


Looking at Figure 4.15b, we immediately recognize that we are not able to show
the relationship one might expect to ﬁnd for the probability of getting caught and
the strictness of laws. The common assumption would be to ﬁnd that stricter laws
correlate with a higher probability of getting caught. This is true for only two out
of the seven discussed epochs, namely for the years of the Nazi regime as well
as the eastern part of Germany during the division of the country. Both eras are
characterized by very strict control mechanisms. For all other epochs, we ﬁnd the
opposite effect: Stricter laws do not increase, but rather decrease the probability
of getting caught. One possible explanation for this phenomenon is that with less
4.6 Finding and Discussing Correlations                                          155

strict laws, the women felt a comparatively lower need to hide their profession
and work hidden from society. Being more open – at least more than during other
epochs – that one offered sexual services led to more and easier arrests. It might,
however, be more reasonable to understand the interactions differently here. For
the ﬁrst years, it seems as if the laws became stricter as a reaction to a low prob-
ability of getting caught rather than the other way around. Perhaps the authorities
reacted to an expansion of the market with stricter laws. During the ﬁrst epoch,
this is certainly true as Röhrmann (1846, p. 225), among others, describes that the
police was not able to supervise the comparatively large market of commercial sex.
The same is true for the second era when the market became even more difﬁcult to
supervise during the years of the First World War (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 391). For the
years since the fall of the Berlin Wall, the probability of getting caught was higher
than one might expect considering the lax laws, especially since the ProstG came
into effect. The higher presence of the Internet,152 however, increases the risk of
being detected for women working in prostitution. In general, we should keep one
ﬁnding in mind from Figure 4.15b: Stricter laws do not necessarily lead to a higher
probability of getting caught.
    Section 4.6.3 discusses the interdependencies with the supply variable again in
length. Therefore, we do not show the dependencies here. To look at correlations
between the price for commercial sex and the strictness of laws does not seem to
be relevant for us. However, the correlation between the price and other variables
should clearly be analyzed.



4.6.2 Price Correlations

Price is one of the most – if not the most – important economic variable. Many
authors, such as Edlund and Korn (2002, p. 182), argue that prostitution is a well
paid profession. Statements on earnings are rare for obvious reasons, but those we
have, for example in Kavemann (2009b, pp. 96), do not allow us to conclude that
women offering commercial sex realize high proﬁts. We have been arguing be-
fore that sex workers should, due to economic theory, earn a higher wage because
their profession is a risky one. Due to this point, looking at possible correlations
between the price and the probability of getting caught seems to be necessary. Be-
sides, the probability of being caught inﬂuences the decision more if a woman has
something to lose if her work becomes public. In our model, this is the possible
loss of reputation for each individual woman. This shall be the second correlation
to look at.

152 Please refer back to Section 4.5.2 for the discussion.

are given in Table 4.9 and Table 4.10. Both tables can be read and interpreted as
the ones given in Section 4.6.1. The very last column in both tables states the co-
ordinates to mark the different epochs in the scatter diagrams. Figure 4.16a shows
the correlation between the price and the possible loss of reputation, whereas Fig-
ure 4.16b displays the correlation between the price and the probability of getting
caught.

We expect that with a higher loss of reputation, the price for commercial sex in-
creases. This should be true as women have to risk more if they lose more in
case of detection. In Figure 4.16a, we see that for the periods one, two, ﬁve, six,
and seven, the higher loss of reputation led to an appropriate increase in the price
variable. During the Nazi regime (epoch three) women were not compensated by
earning more for their huge loss of reputation. The Nazis were, in fact, concerned
about prostitution being too expensive for their soldiers to afford and they did their
best to keep prices low (Timm, 2002, p. 252). In comparison to the possible loss of
reputation, prostitution was expensive on the grounds of the GDR. That was, how-
ever, mainly driven by a low supply (Falck, 1998, p. 19) and less by the standing of
women in society. Here again, we see that the political framework does matter and
the two epochs having a very different system form the exceptions. Summarizing,
it seems that with a higher loss of reputation, the price of commercial sex really
did increase over the years.
In the right panel of Figure 4.16, it is expected that a higher probability of getting
caught induces a higher price. It seems this is not always the case for our data.
During the Nazi regime, the GDR as well as in post-World War II Germany, the

ordering of price and the probability of getting caught shows this reasonable rela-
tionship. During the ﬁrst and second analyzed era, the price was higher than one
might expect when considering the low probability of getting caught. Here, the loss
of reputation seems to have been the driving force of the high price: Women lost
a lot when found out to work as a prostitute and even with the comparatively low
risk they had to bear, it seems that they were still compensated for this possibility.
For the years since the fall of the Berlin Wall, the probability of getting caught
was high compared to the low price. For Hamburg more policemen were found
during these years and, furthermore, we argued that the higher presence of the In-
ternet led to a higher probability of getting caught. It seems that more members of
the online community, however, did not induce a price increase. Again, we were
looking at the price for street prostitution and in comparatively cheap brothels in
Hamburg for the seventh and sixth epoch respectively. This might lead to a rather
low price estimation. In addition, the loss of reputation was comparatively small
during both epochs and there was not much to fear from being caught at all. This
might also explain the less perfect correlation for both eras. Nevertheless, we ﬁnd
that for most epochs, a higher probability of getting caught led to a higher price
for commercial sex.
   The now following Section 4.6.3 discusses the so far missing correlations of
the supply variable. It seems, that supply should be represented on the abscissa as
we are most interested in this variable and the inﬂuence the other parameters have
on its intensity. Due to that, it has been decided to show all supply correlations
together instead of including them in Sections 4.6.1 and 4.6.2.
158                                                       4 An Application of the Meta-Model

4.6.3 Supply Correlations

Using the models developed in Chapter 3, we took a closer look at what inﬂu-
ences the supply from a theoretical perspective. We then evaluated the parameters
identiﬁed to be most inﬂuential and ordered according to their strength over the
epochs. Now we display which of the variables changed the estimated number of
prostitutes.
    We again start with the variable for the strictness of law. Table 4.11 and Figure
4.17a show the relationship of these two variables. What we expect is, in fact, that
a stricter law leads to a lower supply of commercial sex. This correlation cannot
be found for the majority of the epochs. In fact, we only ﬁnd a lower supply with
stricter laws during the Nazi regime and within the GDR – the epochs of limited
inﬂuence for democratically elected representatives. For all other years, the direc-
tion of the inﬂuence seems to be the opposite. In years with high supply on the
commercial sex market, such as the ﬁrst period, governments implemented stricter
laws. It seems that the interaction between both variables is the same as has been
discussed in Section 4.6.1: The legislator rather reacted to a high supply of com-
mercial sex with stricter laws instead of creating an environment that would lead to
a fall in supply. However, with that, the intention of many laws – the containment
of the spread of prostitution – was missed.153 In Section 4.6.1, we saw that stricter
laws led to a higher loss of reputation. Although, the strictness of laws does not
seem to inﬂuence the supply directly, it might through the loss of reputation – the
next variable we look at.
    The coordinates for the variables r and N can be found in Table 4.12 and are
shown in Figure 4.17b. Strict laws led to a higher loss of reputation and vice versa.
Looking at the relationship between the loss of reputation and the supply, we ﬁnd
that in periods with a high possible loss of reputation, the supply was also rather
high. Only for the third and fourth epoch, we ﬁnd the opposite behavior with fewer
women offering sexual services due to higher possible losses. For the other eras, it
seems that a high loss of reputation was a result of a large market for commercial
sex. Society’s disapproval of prostitution does not seem to lead to a lower supply.
Instead, the opposite might be a more promising conclusion: Society’s acceptance
of prostitution as a profession leads to a smaller supply.
    For the relationship between the price and supply we ﬁnd more straightforward
results. We now consider Table 4.13 as well as Figure 4.17c. What we see is that
153 To name just a few examples: The brothel ban in 1846 had the goal to lower the amount of

street prostitution (Hartmann, 2006, pp. 15); demands to eliminate prostitution in general rose
during the discussions on the Lex Heinze (Reichstag, 1898, pp. 397); during the Nazi regime,
street prostitution was supposed to vanish from society (Timm, 2002, p. 237); and during the
GDR one thought that prostitution would not be present at all in a socialist country (Korzilius,
2005, pp. 196, 206, 405, 483).

One has to keep two facts in mind: Firstly, we were only able to ﬁnd a statement
on registered women in Cologne, which might not represent the real situation on
the market. The incentive to hide work from authorities was huge and many women
who were forced to work in prostitution (Paul, 1994, p. 135) are not captured in
any data. Secondly, the prices might seem high in this scatter diagram, but after
recalling our ﬁndings from Section 4.6.2, we know that the Nazis kept prices for
commercial sex low in order to make it affordable for all soldiers (Timm, 2002,
p. 252). As before, data from the Nazi regime has to be treated very carefully to
avoid drawing the wrong conclusions. It has been mentioned already, that during
the GDR, low supply led to a high price (Falck, 1998, p. 19). Furthermore, the price
for bread was not found by market interactions but set by the government – a free

adjustment was not possible and, consequently, a comparison of corresponding

   The last correlation seems the most interesting one to look at, especially when
considering our ﬁndings from Chapter 3. There we saw that in a model including
uncertainty, a higher probability of getting caught should lead to a lower supply of
commercial sex. In fact, we ﬁnd a perfect relationship between these two variables
as shown in Table 4.14 and Figure 4.17d: The ordering of the respective epoch
systematically moved one position further to the right of equation 4.2 – the supply
decreased –, if it has moved one position further to the left in equation 4.9 – the
probability has increased. Equations 3.45, 3.48, as well as 3.75 predict this rela-
tionship. Interestingly, although we do not seem to know what threatens women
about being caught to work in prostitution, we are able to show that the probability
of getting caught affects the supply of commercial sex. To summarize, one can
4.6 Finding and Discussing Correlations                                                       161

state that a higher probability of getting caught led to a lower supply of estimated
prostitutes in Germany for the years since 1846.
    From equation 3.3 in Chapter 3, we assumed that there is a close connection
between the loss of reputation and the probability of getting caught – at least from
the theoretical point of view. Intuitively, it seems reasonable to assume that the
negative effect on the supply of commercial sex caused by an increasing proba-
bility of getting caught, which we are able to show, is even stronger with a higher
loss of reputation. The same should be true for an increasing penalty χ (here not
measured).154 For the data chosen here, however, the loss of reputation and the
probability of getting caught often have very diverse ranks in the ordering and their
effects are seldom working in the same direction. Illustrating how both variables
inﬂuence the supply at the same time is very difﬁcult. Figures 4.18a and 4.18b try
to show all three variables and their interaction in one graph.

                             I III            6 II    (5,2)
                             II VII           5 III   (7,6)
                             IV VI            4 IV    (4,7)
                             V V              3V      (3,3)

In fact, one important conclusion can be drawn from Table 4.15: During epochs
where the loss of reputation and the probability of getting caught were working in
the same direction, the effect on supply was intensiﬁed. This is especially true for
the third and ﬁfth epoch and, to some extend, the fourth epoch. During the other
4.7 Summary of Chapter 4                                                                  163

eras, however, ρ and r work in opposite directions and in those cases ρ was always
dominant in inﬂuencing the supply. This is what we already saw in Figure 4.17d:
A high probability of getting caught always led to a lower supply. The effect of the
loss of reputation is, unfortunately, not easy to capture.
   It is questionable that there is a causal relationship between the loss of reputa-
tion and the probability of getting caught. However, as they seem to act differently
and might inﬂuence the supply directly, we show their interactions in Table 4.16
and Figure 4.19.
