From Figure 4.19, we immediately see that there really seems to be a correlation,
although probably not a causality, between ρ and r. Both variables only work in
the same direction during the third and fourth epoch, which can also be seen very
clearly from Figure 4.19. For all other years, they seem to hinder one another in-
stead of strengthening possible effects on the supply. As the probability of getting
caught seems to be well captured, further research could focus on modeling the
loss of reputation differently in order to show its effect on the supply more accu-
rately.



4.7 Summary of Chapter 4

In Chapter 4, we applied the model we built in Chapter 3 to the past and present
state of prostitution. We identiﬁed ﬁve relevant variables – the strictness of laws,
164                                                 4 An Application of the Meta-Model

the possible loss of reputation, the probability of getting caught, the price for ser-
vices, as well as the supply. We found appropriate measures to put them in an
ordinal order and reviewed the history of prostitution to see whether the measure
chosen reﬂected the situation on the market.
    The strictness of laws has been measured by the number of arrests in Hamburg
due to illegal prostitution. We chose Hamburg as the city’s commercial sex sector
always was and still is one of the biggest in Germany. Furthermore, Hamburg is an
independent state and thus offers individual statistics for most of the parameters.
The loss of reputation was the most difﬁcult variable to measure. To order the
possible loss of reputation, we looked at the number and share of female students
in Germany and in Hamburg. Feminists demand equal rights of schooling among
genders as a necessity to give women a standing in society that men already have.
With an open and equal society, the loss of reputation if working on the market for
commercial sex should be lower. For the probability of getting caught, we took the
number of policemen in Hamburg as an indicator. To be able to order the price, we
measured it relative to one kg of rye bread for each epoch and the supply relative
to the population in Germany.
    After ordering the variables, we then plotted pairs of them to observe any cor-
relations. Most interestingly, we found what Chapter 3 already predicted: A higher
probability of getting caught led to a lower supply of commercial sex. This was,
however, not necessarily true for stricter laws. We were only partly able to explain
the inﬂuence the loss of reputation had on women’s decision to offer commercial
sex in Germany. Further research is needed on this issue. Other suggestions for
further research as well as a critical discussion on the ﬁndings of the work at hand
shall be given in the following Chapter 5. This chapter also marks the end of the
work.
Chapter 5
Summary and Conclusion




The following chapter reviews, summarizes, criticizes, and discusses the results.
Section 5.1 summarizes the previous chapters and gives a short overview of what
was discussed until that point. Section 5.2 focuses on the problems we faced and
those we could not solve – especially regarding both models from Chapter 3 as
well as shortcomings in the data used in Chapter 4. Section 5.3 proposes some
ideas for further research on the topic. The following Section 5.4 focuses on our
ﬁndings and discusses the achievements of the work. Section 5.5 formulates policy
implications and Section 5.6 gives concluding remarks.



5.1 Summarizing the Work

We started the work by taking a closer look at the history of prostitution. The 167
years we analyzed were divided into seven epochs. These epochs were identiﬁed
as they marked either a reform in jurisdiction – and therefore a change in legisla-
tion – or a radical change in society. The reader should be aware that the German
history is a special one full of drastic upheavals and not characterized by a steady
process of liberalization like in many other nations. As a matter of fact, we ana-
lyzed and discussed very different regimes from a monarchy over a democracy to
a dictatorship and then back to a democracy the German history is anything but
boring. The years of separation leave us with two individual states that shared a
country and a language, but were very different in most other characteristics.
   The years between 1846 and the turn of the century served as a starting point.
The country was struggling with its self-image as, on the one hand, the church still
had a huge inﬂuence (Evans, 1976, p. 121), but sexuality, on the other hand, started
to become a constant topic (Lacroix and Helbing, 1995, pp. 122). The ﬁrst epochs

Eva-Maria Heberer, Prostitution, DOI 10.1007/978-3-658-04496-1_5,
© Springer Fachmedien Wiesbaden 2014
166                                                        5 Summary and Conclusion

are characterized by the ﬁght of authorities against the spread of venereal diseases.
Penicillin was not invented yet and an infection often led to the death of the pa-
tient (see, e.g.,Röhrmann (1846, p. 21) or Sauerteig (1999, 34)). One thought that
an increasing rate of infections could only be prevented if the ﬁght against pros-
titutes was successful (Sauerteig, 1999, pp. 14) as they were seen as the major
source (Sauerteig, 2001, p. 77). Especially during war years, the health of the pop-
ulation was worth protecting and an increasing rate of infection was a constant
fear (Reichstag, 1918, pp. 1710). Shortly after closing, brothels were reopened
and frequent health tests again obligatory (Hartmann, 2006, pp. 40). Compared to
the following epochs, prostitution was very present in the society, the supply was
huge (Röhrmann, 1846, pp. 47), and buying commercial sex was very common
(Evans, 1976, p. 106). After the start of the new century, another crime found its
way into the German penal code: pimping. A spectacular trial against the ﬁrst of-
ﬁcial pimp led to many changes in legislation (Lacroix and Helbing, 1995, p. 134)
and was used to limit most forms of art in Germany (Evans, 1976, p. 126). It took
several more years until the handling of sexuality changed. Sex education started
(Sauerteig, 1999, pp. 231) and a general liberalization emerged (Sauerteig, 1999,
p. 226). The GeschlKrG enacted in 1927 ﬁnally led to an actual impunity of pros-
titution (Roos, 2002, p. 71), but also tightened the regulations concerning health
checks for sex workers (Sauerteig, 1999, p. 360). As most legal liberalizations, the
one for prostitution came to an end with Hitler’s seizure of power in 1933.
    For many, the problem of prostitution was not solved with the GeschlKrG,
which helped, among others, the National Socialist Workers’ Party (Nationalsozi-
alistische Deutsche Arbeiterpartei, NSDAP) gain many votes from conservatives
(Roos, 2002, p. 68). The Nazis created a society in which visible prostitution was
completely banned, but women and their services were still provided for many
men (Timm, 2002, p. 242). Women were expected to give birth to new Aryan
soldiers and live a decent life (Timm, 2002, p. 246). Prostitution in general was
seen as a "shame of humanity" (Hitler, 1925, p. 179, own translation), all kinds of
street prostitution were forbidden, and many prostitutes were sent to concentration
camps (Bock, 1980, pp. 83, 96). In contrast, various cities opened licensed brothels
(Roos, 2002, pp. 84) and prostitution had the aim to preserve the purity of blood
(Paul, 1994, pp. 117). As a matter of fact, brothels opened for the forced laborers,
the Wehrmacht, and within concentration camps (see Timm (2002, p. 247), Roos
(2002, p. 88), and Paul (1994, pp. 23)). Years of forced prostitution followed (Paul,
1994, p. 135).
    After the Second World War, the major fear of the Allies was the spread of
venereal disease among their soldiers (Falck, 1998, p. 36). Many women had to
prostitute themselves – mostly for food and not money (Korzilius, 2005, p. 27) –
and infection rates increased rapidly. Eventually, the country was divided into two
parts. The Russian sector and future GDR quickly reached full employment and
5.1 Summarizing the Work                                                           167

started to integrate women in the working population (Korzilius, 2005, p. 21). A
process of equalization emerged (Falck, 1998, p. 50), which did not include the
legal acceptance of prostitution. After 1968, prostitution was completely forbid-
den in the GDR, although often used by the Stasi to collect compromising proof
against special customers (Falck, 1998, p. 114). The ban of prostitution led to very
few suppliers (Fuchs, 1991, p. 96), who were able to realize comparatively high
earnings (Falck, 1998, p. 147). At the same time in the West, women earned much
less if working on the market for commercial sex, but lived in a society that started
to move towards accepting pre-marital sex (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 8) and open
views on sexuality in general. At the end of this process, prostitution was decrimi-
nalized in 1974 and from then on only an administrative offense (Hartmann, 2006,
p. 259). After the fall of the Berlin Wall in late 1989, this law was valid for East
Germany as well.
    For both parts of the country, legislation made every action supporting prostitu-
tion illegal (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 57). In addition, the double standards concern-
ing prostitution were still very present: prostitution was immoral and could not be
called a profession (Leo, 1995, p. 58), health insurance was not available for the
women (Leopold et al., 1994, p. 28), customers could not be forced to pay for a
service (Leo, 1995, pp. 222), income from sex work was taxed as income from ser-
vices (Die Grünen, 1990, p. 8), and all arrangements that created a better and safer
atmosphere for women were forbidden (Gleß, 1999, pp. 104). After the Bundestag
rejected a bill concerning the equalization of prostitution in 1998, a request from
the UN ﬁnally started the process of legislation (Müller, 2008, p. 18) and resulted
in the ProstG in 2002. The goal was to abolish all discrimination against sex work-
ers (Kavemann, 2010, p. 214) and since the law came into effect, prostitution is
legally seen as an autonomous decision for a risky profession (Bundesministerium
für Familie, 2007, p. 6). With that, most of the aforementioned double standards
came to an end, although there is still plenty room for improvement (see, e.g.,
Bundesministerium für Familie (2007, p. 79), Winter (2009, p. 226), as well as
Kavemann and Rabe (2009b, p. 305)). In particular, moral stigmatization of pros-
titution is still present (see, e.g., SOFFI K. (2005b, pp. 200)) and will not vanish
without further work and intervention.
    Having reviewed the changes in laws concerning prostitution, we then took
an economic perspective to analyze them and developed two different models in
Chapter 3. As a ﬁrst step, we used a model based on standard budget constraints,
which is inspired by Wickström and Barthel (2005). We assumed that women can
choose between offering work on the regular labor market – the secure alternative
– or the market for commercial sex – the risky profession. If women decide to ded-
icate at least part of their time on sex work, they risk being detected. This detection
is costly in terms of a loss of reputation. As any kind of loss – and particularly the
loss of reputation – it is much more reasonable for it to have an inﬂuence if there
168                                                         5 Summary and Conclusion

is a future. We therefore introduced savings and an intertemporal model. We used
the Slutsky decomposition to analyze how changes in the probability of getting
caught inﬂuence the working decision of women. Income and substitution effects
were decomposed and we found a rather interesting and very reasonable result:
With a higher probability of getting caught, fewer women were willing to work as
a prostitute.
    In a second step, we used a model developed by Sandmo (1981). This model
was initially created to explain the behavior of tax evaders and we modiﬁed it
to suit our research question. A general von Neumann-Morgenstern utility was
used to integrate riskiness in the model. Besides, women realized different budget
constraints depending on whether they were caught or not. If caught, a penalty
tax was levied on their earnings from sex work, which left them with a disposable
income lower than what they had if they only worked on the regular labor market.
Again, the Slutsky decomposition was used. In this case, we found that women
offered less of their time on the commercial sex sector with a higher probability
of getting caught. This effect grew stronger with a higher penalty tax for being
caught.
    The goal was to show how the legislation inﬂuenced the situation on the market
of commercial sex. In order to do so, four variables were analyzed that we assumed
to have an inﬂuence on the ﬁfth and most interesting variable: the supply.
    For the supply of commercial sex in Germany, we measured the estimated num-
ber of active prostitutes relative to the population in Germany or the respective re-
gion. Based on these calculations, we found that the supply was very high during
the ﬁrst two epochs and very low during the GDR and the Nazi regime. The years
since the reuniﬁcation were placed in the middle of the ordering, with the seventh
epoch having the lowest supply and the ﬁfth epoch the highest supply.
    The price of commercial sex was calculated relative to one kg of rye bread
during the respective years in Germany. Price observations are seldom, but one
statement per epoch could be found. Relative to one kg of bread, prostitution was
most expensive during the GDR and is cheapest today. In between, we have – in
this order – the second, ﬁrst, third, ﬁfth, and sixth epoch.
    To measure the strictness of laws, we used the number of arrests due to ille-
gal prostitution in Hamburg. Hamburg was chosen since forbidden areas are still
present there and, accordingly, data on arrests is at least partly available. By using
the arrests as means of measurement and reviewing the history again critically, we
found that the laws were strictest during the Nazi regime followed by the years of
the GDR. During the last three epochs, a steady decrease of strictness was found.
The years between 1846 and 1932 should be grouped in the medium range.
    Our fourth variable – the possible loss of reputation – was the most difﬁcult
to measure. We used the share of female students in Germany and saw that it ﬁt
reality quite well when looking at the history of prostitution. Further, we were
5.1 Summarizing the Work                                                         169

able to state that women had most to lose during the Nazi regime and the loss is
assumed to be lowest for today. Women’s rights was a very present topic during the
GDR and the loss, therefore, was only in the middle range. The potential loss was
higher during the ﬁrst two epochs and lower in post-World War II West Germany.
    Finally, we chose the number of policemen in Hamburg as an indicator for
the probability of getting caught. The highest probability of getting caught selling
commercial sex was during the GDR, followed by the years of National Socialism.
Today, the higher presence of the Internet induces a higher probability of getting
caught, which gives further justiﬁcation to order the variable for Germany after the
fall of the Berlin Wall on a rather higher position. The ﬁrst years of our analysis
were characterized by a large market of commercial sex and due to that, women
had a better chance to work undetected and the probability of getting caught was,
accordingly, valued lower.
    In a last step, we analyzed the correlations between the relevant variables. In
turn, the price, strictness of law, and supply were deﬁned as independent variables
and we checked whether correlations with the other variables exist. For the strict-
ness of law, we found that the possible loss of reputation increased the stricter the
laws became over the years. A positive correlation between the strictness of laws
and the probability of getting caught could not be shown. In contrast, we found
that the probability of getting caught for most epochs was lower for stricter laws.
One explanation might be that women were less careful in periods with lax laws.
    As a second independent variable, the price for commercial sex was deﬁned.
There, as well, we looked for correlations with the loss of reputation and the prob-
ability of getting caught. For the majority of the epochs, we were able to show that
the price increased with a higher loss of reputation. The years of National Social-
ism and the GDR did not follow this rule. For both of these epochs, it is reasonable
to assume that factors other than pure market interactions had a huge inﬂuence. An
increasing probability of getting caught did not necessarily lead to a higher price
of commercial sex. Here it seems that the loss of reputation and not the probability
of getting caught was responsible for price changes.
    Finally, we looked at correlations regarding the supply variable: Stricter laws
did not lead to a lower supply, but rather seem to be the reaction to a large mar-
ket for commercial sex. Similarly, we found that the supply was mostly high in
epochs with a higher possible loss of reputation. Again, it seems more reasonable
to assume that the loss of reputation increased as a reaction on a high supply. For
the remaining two variables, more straightforward correlations could be proven: A
higher price led to a higher supply – again, the years of the Nazi regime and the
GDR are exceptions – and a higher probability of getting caught had the opposite
effect. Both relationships are in line with our expectations.
    From the theoretic model developed and discussed in Chapter 3, we concluded
that a higher probability of getting caught led to a lower supply of commercial
170                                                        5 Summary and Conclusion

sex in Germany. In addition, it seems reasonable to assume that this effect should
be strengthened during those epochs characterized by a high risk and a high loss
of reputation. We tried to prove whether this is true for the data chosen here. We
found that the effect on the supply intensiﬁed in the epochs in which both vari-
ables worked in the same direction. However, during years of opposed effects, the
strength of the probability of getting caught dominated over the effect of the loss
of reputation. The assumed strong relationship between the risk and the supply in
theory could, therefore, be shown using the chosen data.



5.2 Shortcomings in the Model and the Data

Having summarized the entire work in Section 5.1, we now focus on certain short-
comings. Some of them have been addressed already in Chapter 3 and Chapter 4
respectively. It seems, however, necessary to review the most important ones in
order to ﬁnd starting points for further research.
    First and foremost, one has to be aware that both models applied are only partly
able to reﬂect a woman’s decision. The loss of reputation had to be simpliﬁed to a
large extend. Whether it depends on other factors, such as the age of the women,
their level of education, or the social group they belong to, is questionable. All
these might inﬂuence the level of loss and their integration could lead to more ap-
propriate results. One has to admit that the generality of the budget constraints,
although having many advantages, also prevents more concrete statements. Look-
ing at speciﬁed utility functions might lead to closer predictions of the behavior
of agents. The model by Sandmo (1981) includes risk and with that it also allows
further conclusions. Nevertheless, the loss of reputation could be included with the
interactions of other variables instead of assuming it to be a constant. In doing so,
it would not drop out in the derivatives and gain a certain kind of impact. Also
limiting our ﬁndings from both models is the fact that we look at the decision to
engage in the market for commercial sex as a supplier only. So far, we have not
taken the decision to demand services into account, but rather kept it constant.
Neither did we develop an equilibrium for this market. Finally, we cannot deny the
critique most people have about economics: We construct a model with mathemat-
ical equations assuming that we are able to predict the decision of people. But of
course, the individuality of each person cannot be reﬂected with a model. We can
try to adopt certain characteristics, such as risk aversion, but we always have to
draw our conclusion for all, although we just look at one representative agent.
    Shortcomings in the data are especially present in Chapter 4. The data we have
used is seldom complete and often not fully comparable. First and foremost, Ham-
burg is, of course, not the entire Germany. As many laws are in the responsibility
5.2 Shortcomings in the Model and the Data                                       171

of the states, the treatment of prostitution was very diverse throughout the country:
What was valid in the North might have been treated very differently in the South
of Germany. We can only hope that the data found for Hamburg is able to draw a
general picture of the situation in Germany, which can be applied also for the other
states. The analysis is further complicated by the fact that many data points are
missing. Often, the time series are only available for some years and for all other
periods we had to use statements from contemporary witnesses or from publica-
tions which are not completely objective. To name just a few, Röhrmann (1846),
Jeannel (1869), Paul (1994), and Falck (1998) wrote very informative statements
on the topic of prostitution. But in each of the mentioned publications, the reader
feels that the topic is an emotional one for the respective author. Of course, this
should happen to most sympathetic persons – and is to some extent also true for the
work at hand – but it leads to problems of comparability and trustworthiness. The
descriptions might mislead when it comes to judging the situation during certain
epochs.
    In addition, problems arise with each indicator individually. The supply is dif-
ﬁcult to measure for obvious reasons. The incentives certainly ﬂuctuate over time,
but women actually never had an incentive to admit what they do for a living. It
is reasonable to assume that only the smallest fraction of active prostitutes ofﬁ-
cially registered in Hamburg (see, e.g., Reichstag (1893, p. 931)). That was one
reason for looking at the estimated number of active prostitutes for each epoch.
Here again, the problem of having to rely on contemporary witnesses arises. In ad-
dition, reliable statements are rare and hard to make especially for the last epochs
(Leopold et al., 1994, p. 255).
    One would think that ordering the price is much more straightforward. Look-
ing at the price for prostitution is, however, quite difﬁcult. Firstly, we can never
be sure whether the statement found is true or not. The incentives to state either
higher or lower earnings are quite large and rather obvious (Kavemann, 2009b, p.
97). Secondly, we might have compared prices for different services: Neither are
all services priced equally nor do all women work in the same sector. We have
discussed before that earning opportunities are quite different between working as
an escort, in a brothel, or on the streets. In addition, even with the measurement
chosen – the price of rye bread – problems arise as we were not able to ﬁnd a
complete time series. The data provided by Hoffmann (1965) is given in DM –
the applied exchange rates are, unfortunately, not given. The same is true for the
data stated in e provided by the Statistisches Bundesamt (2004). These prices are
not the observed prices, but data used to calculate the consumer price index. How
much they differ from the actual price of rye bread is unknown. For most recent
years, the Statistisches Bundesamt does not give actual prices. The only data avail-
able is for the consumer price index, which we used to calculate the most likely
prices. As only some statements on prices were compared, we were able to ﬁnd
172                                                         5 Summary and Conclusion

statements in the same currency for most years in the annual abstract of statistics.
Data for a different year had to be used only for the ﬁrst and third epoch. During
the GDR, the price for bread was set by the government. Accordingly, it cannot be
compared to a price found by market interactions.
    The changes in law have been measured by the number of arrests in Hamburg.
Firstly, we have to admit that measuring the strictness of laws by the arrests due
to one crime is questionable. As stricter laws are not necessarily correlated with
more policemen, an increasing number of arrests might be not possible to achieve
even with this increased strictness. Other factors also play a role. For example,
stricter laws could have led to the prostitutes’ decision to work more discreetly to
avoid being caught. The data we used for the years after 1971 was provided by the
Polizei Hamburg (2013) and includes arrests in Hamburg for illegal prostitution.
The numbers are, unfortunately, not available for §184e StGB individually, but in-
clude all crimes forbidden by §§180, 180a, 181a, 184, 184a, 184b, 184c, 184d, and
184e StGB until 2007. This classiﬁcation changed after 2007 and now addition-
ally includes §184f StGB, which makes the data less comparable. As a matter of
fact, the data given in Appendix A.4 should be treated very carefully. Not only did
measurement errors arise due to the inclusion of many crimes and the exclusion
of indoor prostitution from the data, but also due to the changes in the deﬁnitions
over time.
    In order to measure the loss of reputation, we looked at the share of female
students in Germany. The basic idea is to measure what women might lose if be-
ing caught selling commercial sex. In general, this variable is a rather soft one and,
therefore, almost impossible to measure. Due to that, a somewhat abstract measure
had to be taken and the number of female students has been chosen. The reason
was that equal chances in education for both sexes were a central demand of the
equalization movement since its very beginning (see, e.g., Nave-Herz (1997, p. 7)
and Die Vierte Weltfrauenkonferenz (1995)). As both variables – the share of fe-
male students and the loss of reputation – probably follow a time-trend, reasonable
conclusions can be drawn.
    The probability of getting caught was evaluated by looking at the number of
policemen in Hamburg. Data is not even available for all years for this variable.
The years after the Second World War were prepared by the Statistisches Amt für
Hamburg und Schleswig-Holstein (2013a), but for earlier years, we had to look
at the annual abstract of statistics. Yearbooks before 1874 are not available and
the ﬁrst number stated on the size of the police force is given in 1914. Although
the population census is available for earlier years, it arranges the population with
respect to work place, and many different professions are listed – the policemen
do not seem to be counted individually. The most important critique, however,
is that more policemen does not necessarily mean that the focus of them was on
the market for commercial sex. In the data, we cannot distinguish between trafﬁc
5.3 Proposals for Further Research                                                173

policemen and members of the vice squad. Also, the probability of getting caught
should be understood not only in terms of a possible arrest, but rather that the
majority of women might care more about friends and acquaintance ﬁnding out
than an arrest by the police. Unfortunately, we are not able to measure the risk of
being detected by a family member.
    Furthermore, we are not able to state whether we see a correlation or a causal-
ity between the variables. The relationship between the loss of reputation and the
probability of getting caught should, for example, only show a correlation. For the
probability of getting caught and the supply, it seems that a causality was found.
However, a certain statement cannot be made. In addition, we had to make an as-
sumption if a variable is independent or dependent to be able to draw the scatter
diagrams. This relationship can sometimes be the other way around. For the prob-
ability of getting caught and the strictness of laws, for example, the dependencies
could also be opposite, which would then lead to more reasonable results. The
same is true for the supply and the loss of reputation as well as the strictness of
laws. Finally, Chapter 3 concluded that a higher loss of reputation increases the
negative effect that a higher probability of getting caught has on supply. This can
only be proven for those epochs in which both variables work in the same direc-
tion. For all other epochs, the probability of getting caught is the driving force and
dominates the effect of the loss of reputation.



5.3 Proposals for Further Research

The Bundesministerium für Familie constantly carries out research studies focus-
ing on the market for commercial sex. Currently, a project for women who wish
to leave the sector is ﬁnanced and a group of well-known researchers is working
on its scientiﬁc documentation. However, research on the statistical characteristics
of the market for commercial sex often lacks completeness as the market is just
too big, too closed, and almost impossible to enter as a stranger. The will of the
legislator to gain further insight should be expressed by easier funding for more
projects. From an economic point of view, it would be easier to understand the
interactions of the market if further data was available. Several indicators of in-
terest include: the wages earned on the market for commercial sex and those of
possible outside options, what women claim to lose if caught, if they care about
being caught by the police at all, how they reacted to recent changes in jurisdic-
tion, etc. The study by SOFFI K. (2005b) already addresses most of the mentioned
variables. However, the number of interviewed sex workers is, compared to the
number of women working in this sector, rather small. Nevertheless, compared to
other studies, it is one of the biggest. An additional study more than ten years after
174                                                        5 Summary and Conclusion

the ProstG was enacted should give us further insight as the market had more time
to adopt to the new circumstances.
   The models used seem to match the data quite well and show reasonable in-
teractions when it comes to the work decision of the women. However, further
research on the loss of reputation seems to be promising. Della Giusta et al. (2009)
already point out that this variable should be of major interest and we certainly
agree. This is crucial not only for improving a theoretical economic model, but
also for supporting the process of equalization of sex workers. It might also be
interesting to look at different utility functions and determine which type best ex-
plains the decision of women to engage in the market for commercial sex.
   With an improved model, one could focus again on ﬁnding other and maybe
more suitable indicators. The loss of reputation is in particular, an indicator wor-
thy of improvement. The probability of getting caught should also be reconsidered
– to ﬁnd a way of integrating a woman’s peer group and the risk of being detected
would be interesting for further research. In general, a lack of data seems to be the
central problem as not even the variables that are comparatively easier to measure
were accessible and complete. The work at hand tried to reach the goal to sum-
marize the relevant data sources and literature to give further research a ground to
build up on. All data used is given in Appendix A and is, of course, also available
upon request. Finally, the discussion on correlations and causalities is worth car-
rying on. Which variables have a direct inﬂuence on others should be discussed
further, as conclusions about the effects of certain changes in legislation might
then be possible. In Sweden and France, for example, stricter laws are seen as the
reason for a lower supply of street prostitution (see, e.g., Kavemann (2009a, p.
26), Dodillet and Östergren (2011, pp. 8), and Tränkle (2009, p. 74)). However,
legislators still need to prove that the supply really decreased and not just move
out of sight.



5.4 Achievements and Contributions

The most important achievement of the work at hand should be seen as bringing
the topic of prostitution back to people’s – and especially economists – mind. Of
course other papers like Della Giusta et al. (2009), Edlund and Korn (2002), as
well as Arunachalam and Shah (2008) already point out that the commercial sex
market realizes huge proﬁts and contributes to the GDP and yet we have rather
little knowledge about it. However, the vast majority of economists refrain from
analyzing the commercial sex sector. As in all other shadow markets, variables
are difﬁcult to describe and to measure. This is probably the reason for not carry-
ing out further research on this topic. A lack of interest cannot be assumed taking
5.4 Achievements and Contributions                                                         175

into consideration how broad the feedback was given to recent literature among
economists, especially Edlund and Korn (2002). Unfortunately, difﬁcult topics are
often avoided by researchers as working on them is much more time consuming
and time is often a very scarce resource in science. However, if other publications
on the market for commercial sex follow, knowledge can grow and the analysis
can become more trustworthy. The work at hand tries to make the market for com-
mercial sex, the considerations of agents, as well as the interactions between them
understandable and comprehensible for a broad audience.
   To understand a market and its participants, a glance into the history is often
essential. Researchers tend to turn their attention on new phenomenon and recent
developments and forget that the history might already show similar events and –
more importantly – provide solutions to known problems. To our best knowledge,
no work has been published before that looks at changes in the society, legislation,
and economic variables at the same time for such a long period of time in Ger-
many. Most of the literature reviewed in this paper analyzes certain epochs rather
than giving an overview and others focus only on questions concerning one of
the areas of interest. To name just a few, Hartmann (2006), Gleß (1999), and von
Galen (2004) focus on legislation concerning prostitution and related crimes, Falck
(1998), Korzilius (2005), and Paul (1994) only discuss narrowly deﬁned epochs,
Edlund and Korn (2002), Arunachalam and Shah (2008), as well as Della Giusta
et al. (2009) draw their attention to economic considerations. As the interaction
between the areas and developments over time are crucial for understanding, the
broader view of this work contributes to the literature immensely.
   Edlund and Korn (2002) and Della Giusta et al. (2009) are probably the most
cited publications on the economic theory behind the market for commercial sex.
Both papers’ contributions should not be underestimated, but they also show short-
comings that we tried to avoid in our models.
   It seems that Edlund and Korn (2002) lost themselves in many assumptions,
which do not contribute much to the understanding of the market. First and fore-
most, Edlund and Korn (2002, p. 186) assume that a woman can only choose be-
tween marriage or prostitution. Even ignoring the doubts that immediately come to
one’s mind about whether all single women offer commercial sex,156 the statistics
on prostitution in Germany do not verify such a simpliﬁcation: SOFFI K. (2005b,
p. 192) ﬁnds in a study on the new prostitution law in Germany that 21.9% of all
prostitutes interviewed were married at that time. In addition, Steffan and Kerschl
(2004, p. 37) state that only one third of the interviewed prostitutes were single.

156 A conclusion Edlund and Korn (2002, p. 211) do not want to be drawn from their assumption:

"They may have elected to stay unmarried for reasons exogenous to the model or they may intend
to marry. Therefore, the predictions of the model should be interpreted in terms of predictions
about prostitution rather than about marriage."
176                                                              5 Summary and Conclusion

The rest were either married, divorced, or widowed implying that the majority of
prostitutes were married at least once.
   Further, Edlund and Korn (2002) assume at the very beginning of their publica-
tion that "prostitution is low-skill, labor intensive, female, and well paid" (Edlund
and Korn, 2002, p. 181).157 It has been discussed already in Chapter 3 that the
skills of sex workers do not seem to differ too much from those of the majority of
the population. Besides, Chapter 4 discussed the wage women earn if working in
prostitution and it seems rather questionable whether this can be considered well
paid. A study by Levitt and Venkatesh (2007) ﬁnds that women working on the
streets earn less than 20,000 $ per year (Levitt and Venkatesh, 2007, p. 3). At ﬁrst
sight, this seems to be a high wage compared to the wage a women might earn on
the regular labor market. However, these women have to face an enormous risk of
robbery and violence and should, according to economic theory, earn a compen-
sation for it. 41% of interviewed German prostitutes were victims of their client’s
violence (Müller and Schöttle, 2004, p. 23). These ﬁndnings call for the conclu-
sion that prostitutes do not need compensation for foregone marriage possibilities,
but a compensation for the risks they fear at work.
   In contrast to the models at hand, Edlund and Korn (2002) focus on the demand
of commercial sex as a driving force. In fact, with the explanation that men have
some kind of market power in the market for commercial sex although demand
is not scarce, one could understand such a focus. But the decision of women is
not speciﬁed and the conclusions from the developed model, accordingly, rather
incomplete. Della Giusta et al. (2009), in contrast, allow the consumers as well as
the suppliers to ﬁnd their optimal consumption bundle through utility maximiza-
tion. Unfortunately, the market clearing supply is never stated explicitly and its
reaction on changes remains ambiguous (Della Giusta et al., 2009, p. 513).
   Besides, the paper by Della Giusta et al. (2009, p. 503) differs from other pub-
lications on the topic by two assumptions. Firstly, they assume that selling sex is
not exclusively a female business and that demand does not only come from men.
The second assumption that distinguishes Della Giusta et al. (2009) from previ-
ous papers is the introduction of norms – an assumption we also included in our
considerations. It cannot be questioned that the commercial sex sector has to ﬁght
against stigma and maliciousness, but Della Giusta et al. (2009) do not allow for
undetected sex work, which seems to contradict reality. If it were possible to tell
a prostitute by the look on someone’s face then fast decrease of the supply would
probably be the immediate consequence.


157 Certainly arguments can be found that prostitution really is a profession demanding lower

skills, but Edlund and Korn (2002, pp. 188) seem to be rather talking about the education of
women offering sex work than they talk about the actual demands this profession calls for.
5.5 Policy Implications                                                             177

    In contrast to these models, both of our approaches seem to succeed in showing
the reasonable considerations of women who take sex work as a work opportunity
into account. An outside option was included for women leading to a much more
realistic picture of the abilities of women offering sex work. With the probability
of getting caught, risk was included in the model to show how this might inﬂuence
an agent’s decision. In addition, the models were kept as general and straightfor-
ward as possible to create a benchmark for many agents and to avoid unnecessary
complications. Binswanger (2012) has a point in criticizing that the majority of
economists focus more on building up very complex models from a mathematical
point of view instead of focusing on the statements one wants to make. This paper
tries to avoid this. The German legislator deﬁnes prostitution as an autonomous de-
cision for a risky profession (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 6) and we
model it exactly like that. Finally, the theoretical starting point allows us to state
testable assumptions on the behavior of women. Using the ﬁndings from Chapter
3, we are able to ﬁnd measures for the variables of interest and show that both
models predict the behavior of women quite reasonably.
    It has been pointed out before that the measures chosen are debatable and other
variables also might have an inﬂuence. Nevertheless, an appropriate indicator was
suggested for all variables we assumed to have an impact on the supply of com-
mercial sex. Furthermore, data for all these indicators was collected, despite many
difﬁculties. Often the statistical ofﬁces tried to assist and provided as much data
as they had available. However, especially for the years before the Second World
War, historic statistical yearbooks had to be found and considered. For some in-
dicators, such as the number of policemen or female students in Hamburg, one
might think that data is readily available. Unfortunately, this was not the case and
the data had to be collected by hand. Additionally, other sources were found and,
if possible, veriﬁed, especially for the very ﬁrst years of analysis. In total, what the
work at hand achieved was to make variables measurable, which were previously
assumed unmeasurable. Further research might ﬁnd different indicators, but the
ones chosen here seem to provide a good starting point.



5.5 Policy Implications

For the work at hand, the supply of commercial sex served as an indicator to dis-
cuss and evaluate changes in legislation in Germany for the last 167 years. Espe-
cially in Section 4.6, we showed that different variables inﬂuence and drive the
decision of women to offer services. To give any kind of recommendation for de-
cision makers in government is especially difﬁcult for one reason: There is no
consensus in what goal shall be reached. In Sweden and France, the goal was, in
178                                                                  5 Summary and Conclusion

fact, to abolish prostitution in general (see, e.g., Kavemann (2009a, pp. 24) and
Tränkle (2009, p. 69)), whereas the German government claims that prostitution’s
right to exist is not questioned at all (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 6).
Ergo, as long as there is no agreement on whether society’s goal is to lower the
number of prostitutes or not (Bundesministerium für Familie, 2007, p. 8), policy
implications need to be given carefully.
    In the following, we assume that the policy makers intend to lower the supply
–158 not only for the sake of less prostitution in a country but also to ensure higher
prices for services and, accordingly, improve the situation for active sex work-
ers.159 One should be aware that the situation on the market for commercial sex
highly depends on what kind of political system forms the framework. Through-
out the entire analysis, it became obvious that the years of the Nazi regime and the
GDR formed the steady outliers and the predictions are not robust against drastic
changes in the structure of a country. Besides, for all other epochs we ﬁnd that
stricter laws are not correlated with a lower supply, although many decision mak-
ers seem to draw that conclusion. In contrast, Figure 4.17a shows that stricter laws
came with a higher supply throughout history.
    When it comes to the attitude of society towards the market for commercial
sex, it is interesting to note that a low supply of sex work came – for the majority
of the analyzed epochs – with a lower loss of reputation. Accordingly, creating a
society which accepts prostitution as a voluntary decision of women and lowering
the stigmatization of this profession ensures the existence of a comparatively small
market. Besides, Figure 4.15a suggests that stricter laws are often accountable for
a high loss of reputation. Also here, tolerant laws seem to be desirable for many
policy makers.
    Highly correlated are the supply of commercial sex and the probability of get-
ting caught. In Figure 4.17d we show that the higher the probability of getting
caught was ranked, the less sex work was offered. One possible conclusion from
that is that a society only needs to make sure that detection is very likely to de-
crease the supply of commercial sex. However, this conclusion does not seem to
tell the whole story. Keeping in mind that we did not only measure the probability
of being arrested here, we cannot make a statement about how this relationship
behaves with more tolerant friends and family. Because that is exactly what it
comes down to: Women do not fear being detected to work in prostitution due to

158 It has been mentioned in Section 4.6.3 that this was often deﬁned as the goal of policy reforms

(for different policies, see, e.g., Hartmann (2006, pp. 15), Reichstag (1898, pp. 397), Timm (2002,
p. 237), and Korzilius (2005, pp. 196, 206, 405, 483)).
159 Please note that we show in Figure 4.17c that the higher the price for commercial sex the

higher the supply. The sole conclusion from that is that more women are willing to offer services
with increasing prices and by no means that the equilibrium price of commercial sex increases
with a higher supply.
5.6 Concluding Remarks                                                             179

legal consequences but because they are afraid of a moral stigma. How the corre-
lation between the probability of getting caught and the supply of commercial sex
changes if women do not need to fear a moral stigma, is – at that point – hard to
say.
    As long as society has not agreed upon which goal is the one to achieve, policy
implications need to remain rather vague (Kavemann and Rabe, 2009b, p. 309).
What is, however, important to note is that the decriminalization of prostitution
seems to be the right path to follow (Kavemann and Steffan, 2013, pp. 9). Current
media coverage tries to suggest that policy forms of criminalized prostitution – be
it customer or sex worker criminalization – are preferable (see, e.g., Meyer et al.
(2013)). Unfortunately, the grounds for such conclusions are seldom scientiﬁc or
free of a moral judgment. Both do not seem to be the best base for jurisdiction and
are hopefully not path breaking for decision makers in politics.



5.6 Concluding Remarks

Having worked more than four years on a topic like prostitution, the concluding
remarks are the most difﬁcult to write. The topic chosen is anything but usual for a
thesis and it has caused many different reactions over the years. Mostly disbelief.
Out of politeness, the majority of people ask about the topic of a dissertation if
they know someone is in the process of writing. No one expects to hear that the
topic is prostitution in Germany. A puzzled Really?! is the most common reaction
followed by ashamed laughter. Then, most people are interested, ask questions,
and no one really believes that this work will ever be written. Well, it is.
    Writing such a thesis and discussing the topic so often, one of the major goals
was reached – at least some people started to rethink their opinion about pros-
titution. Probably only very few have changed their mind, but I know of one or
two who look differently at women working on the streets. They are not seen as
pitiful and of minor value, but as human beings – and that was at least one aim
of this thesis. Prostitution is a profession, it is not a characteristic or what deﬁnes
the womens’ existence. Of course, the majority have probably not chosen the job
completely voluntarily. Many saw no other way out of debt and misery. Others
have chosen this profession because they wanted to. Neither the ﬁrst nor the sec-
ond type of sex worker deserves to be treated as lepers from society. One should
rather accept the decision made by the person of age and, at the same time, try to
help those who want to change their profession.
    From my point of view, the most important contribution of the thesis at hand
is to provide very different views on prostitution and to analyze the interactions
across the disciplines. Of course, this caused various problems and made working
180                                                       5 Summary and Conclusion

on the topic more difﬁcult. However, a straightforward topic was intentionally not
chosen. I did not choose a topic that allows only to talk about hard economic facts,
but rather one that demands for a different way to analyze and discuss. A pub-
lication which is readable, understandable, and – most importantly – interesting
for many was the goal of this paper and readers should judge whether this goal
was achieved. I am very much looking forward to further discussions and critiques
regarding this topic.
